Inside this circle is a "Pelican in her piety," a bird wounding her own breast to feed her young with her blood, which in alchemy symbolizes the "Great Work" and self-sacrifice. The scene is flanked by the sun and a crescent moon, representing the union of opposites. At the bottom is a square pedestal decorated with four roses, a motif frequently associated with Rosicrucianism.</note>

<meta>This page serves as the preface to an alchemical treatise. The author explains his intent to provide a clear, chaptered commentary on an older, faded manuscript, using the works of the famous alchemist Michael Sendivogius as a guide to uncover the "true sense" of the work rather than its literal, cryptic phrasing. The catchword "legt" at the bottom indicates the final word "ausgelegt" (explained or interpreted) continues on the next page.</meta>

# PREFACE.
<note>original: "PRÆFATIO"</note>

**S**o that I might truly and well deserve the favor of those upright, God- and Art-loving *sons of Doctrine* <term>Latin: filios Doctrinæ; a traditional term for initiates or dedicated students of alchemy</term>, and so that they—being thoroughly instructed in this high secret of the *Hermetic miracle* <note>original: "Hermetischen miraculi"; this refers to the "Great Work" of alchemy, the creation of the Philosopher's Stone</note>—might not spend their time in vain, whether in *study and reading* <note>original: "Studii & lectionis"</note> or in *practice and labor* <note>original: "Practic und laboris"; "labor" specifically refers to the physical work performed at the alchemical furnace</note>; I have seen fit to present the German text, just as it was attached at the end to the following figures in a nearly faded and difficult-to-read script. I have set it forth piece by piece, yet completely and in an orderly sequence, divided into specific *chapters*. Upon this follows my *addition* and explanation, with testimonies from *Sendivogius’s Chemical Light* <note>Referring to Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a world-renowned alchemist whose "New Chemical Light" (Novum Lumen Chymicum) was a foundational text for 17th-century science</note>, drawn *from the forces of Nature* <note>original: "è Naturæ viribus"</note>, and understood according to the *true sense of the Author* <note>original: "& vero Authoris sensu"</note>, *not by the syllables* <note>original: "non Syllabis"</note> *nor the twisted bark of words* <note>original: "aut torto verborum cortice"; a common alchemical idiom warning the reader not to take the metaphors too literally or superficially</note>, understood and ex... <note>The text breaks mid-word; based on the catchword "legt," the full word is "ausgelegt," meaning interpreted or explained.</note>

<summary>In this preface, the author introduces a commentary on a rare, faded alchemical manuscript, promising to explain its "Hermetic miracle" through the proven theories of Michael Sendivogius. He emphasizes understanding the author's true intent rather than getting lost in the cryptic, literal language of the original text.</summary>

<keywords>Preface, Sons of Doctrine, Hermetic miracle, Alchemy, Michael Sendivogius, Chemical Light, Interpretation, Alchemical practice</keywords>

<note>original: "PRÆFATIO"</note>

explained <note>completing the word "ausgelegt" from the previous page</note>, should necessarily follow, so that a beginning may be made in these matters. It likewise seemed good to me to allow a general *discourse* <term>Latin: discursum; a formal discussion or treatise</term> on this subject to precede the rest, so that the *specifics* <note>original: "Specialia"</note> following it might be carried out in a more orderly, understandable, and clear manner.

*Here begins the general discourse on the primary matter* <term>Latin: materia prima; the formless base substance from which all things are created</term> *of metals. Likewise on the essential parts of the Philosopher's Stone.* <note>original: "Lapidis Philosophici"</note>

Accordingly, the mentioned text—as it was attached to the following figures (*with some few corrections and insertions of things that either could not be read or were omitted*)—begins

->with the following words<-
->thus:<-

When the ancient *Philosophers* <note>In alchemical texts, "Philosophers" refers specifically to the "Sons of Art" or alchemists who sought the secrets of nature</note> deeply took to heart the general misery stemming from the *Fall of Adam* <note>original: "Fall Adæ"; in alchemical thought, the Fall corrupted both man and nature, and alchemy was seen as a way to restore both to their original perfection</note> and the curse that followed: They searched through and investigated all of God’s creatures to see if they might find something with which—even if temporal death cannot be entirely resisted—they might nevertheless [remedy] the [difficulties...] <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "schwer-"</note>

[remedy the] difficult afflictions <note>original: "schwerlichen Zufällen"</note> that befall both body and mind, lightening and softening them; and thus, in this vale of tears <term>German: "Jammerthal"; a common historical and biblical expression for the world as a place of sorrow and trial</term>, they might sustain and conclude this short life with bodily health and delightful joy of the mind—found through the graceful and lovely knowledge of God and the investigation of His wonders within created nature—and finally with honest, necessary sustenance that is not burdensome to fellow humans <note>original: "NebenMenschen"</note>, but rather helpful and serviceable.

For this purpose, they have found no nobler or better *subject* <term>Latin: "Subjectum"; the physical matter or material being acted upon</term> than gold, nor any more serviceable or worthy art than *Hermetic Pyrotechnia* <term>The Hermetic "fire-art," a technical name for alchemy</term>. This is because among all creatures under the sun, none is found more stable or more complete than gold. # PREFACE.

->PRÆFATIO.<-

[of] its own **metallic kind** <term>German: "Metallischen Geschlechts"; referring to the shared lineage or nature of all metals</term>, to serve powerfully and mightily, as they have argued and concluded. However, because its bonds are so strong and firm, entwined and locked together in such a way that it is not possible for Nature alone to break it open again for our use (for although she breaks down and destroys all things she gives birth to—as is clearly seen in the degradation of metals in the **bowels of the earth** <term>Latin: "visceribus terræ"</term>—yet such natural **destruction of gold** and **decaying** <term>German: "Vermoderung"</term> as occurs in the earth is unserviceable for medicine), and yet, if left undissolved and in its simple perfection, it is useful and fit for neither the one nor the other indicated intent of the **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers specifically to the Alchemists or "Natural Philosophers" of the time.</note>. 

Therefore, they have sought—through Art, yet with the help of Nature, and through no other than the ingenious **art of fire** <term>German: "Feuer-Kunst"</term>, **Alchemy** <term>Latin: "Alchimiam"</term>, which is to be regarded as almost a mother to all other arts—to dismantle and unlock it, and also by means of a new birth to exalt it and **perfect it beyond perfection** <term>Latin: "plus quam perficiren"; a technical alchemical term for raising a substance to a state of potency greater than what Nature provides</term>,

sich <note>catchword: "itself"</note>

# PREFACE.

->PRÆFATIO.<-

...applied themselves and finally, after many endured labors and dangers, as well as suffered shame and mockery, along with other obstacles and hindrances to which this **Art** <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy, often called the "Sacred Art" or "Great Work."</note> is subject (**For things that are beautiful are difficult** <note>original Latin: "Difficilia enim quæ pulchra"</note>, etc.), through the grace of God, they joyfully achieved their wish and intent.

And accordingly, they presumably inferred and concluded—since no opening may be performed without its appropriate key—that gold can be naturally opened and improved by no other thing than its own and first mother, in and from which it was initially generated, which they called by its right name: **prime matter** <term>Latin: "primam materiam"; the original, undifferentiated substance from which all things were thought to be created</term>.

Thus, they meditated deeply upon the **generation of gold and all metals** <term>In the early modern view, metals "grew" inside the earth like plants</term> and found that this same **prime matter** is partly heavenly and partly earthly; they called the former the **Above** and the latter the **Below**. They also considered that both things have toward each other an equality and inclination, a desire and love—

# PREFACE.

->PRÆFATIO.<-

...must have and bear; also that the **Below** (which they called the **Woman**) should hold itself in natural grace toward the upper **Firmament** <term>The heavens or sky, viewed by alchemists as a physical sphere of influence</term> (which they called the **Man**), and that neither can act without the other. Therefore, the heavenly **rays** <note>original: "Radii"</note> (the first part)—which, through the **conjunction** <note>The alignment of stars or planets</note> of the stars, shoot from the upper firmament onto the earth, and then through the **pores** <note>original: "Poros"</note> of the earth down to the **center of the earth** <note>original: "centrum terræ"</note>—are to be considered the **active principle** <term>Latin: "agens"; the force that initiates action or change</term>. These rays are then received by the **Terrestrial Archeus** <term>The vital, "inner craftsman" or spirit believed to govern the formation of minerals within the earth</term>, who, through its swift motion, drives them back upward to mix with the **earthly liquid substance** (the other part), acting as the **passive principle** <term>Latin: "patiente"; the material that is acted upon</term>. According to the different nature and quality of the various places in the earth which harmonize with the **influence of that star** <note>original: "Astri influens"</note>, they give birth to various forms of **metals and minerals**.

# PREFACE.

->PRÆFATIO.<-

...or noise it dissolves; indeed, it does so as sweetly and naturally as ice melts by the benefit of warm water. If you have found this, you possess the thing from which gold is produced by nature. And although all metals and all things have their origin from it, nothing is so befriended to it as gold. For impurity adheres to other things, but to gold, none! Therefore, it is like a mother to it.

## Treatise 3.
## Tractat. 3.

The first matter <note>original: "materia"</note> of metals is twofold. But one without the other does not create a metal. The first and principal is the **Moisture** <note>original: "Humidum"</note> mixed with the heat of the air. This the **Philosophers** <note>A term used for alchemists or seekers of natural wisdom</note> called **Mercury** <term>In alchemy, Mercury represents the fluid, transformative, and feminine principle of matter</term>, which is governed by the rays of the Sun and Moon in the **Philosophical Sea**. The second is the dry quality of the earth, which they called **Sulphur** <term>The masculine, combustible, and stabilizing principle that provides form to the fluid Mercury</term>.

## Treatise 4.
## Tract. 4.

After the **elements** <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water</note> cast their virtues into the center of the earth, the **Archeus** <term>The "inner craftsman" or vital spirit believed to govern the formation of minerals within the earth</term>—through distilling by the heat of perpetual motion—sublimates <note>To turn a solid into vapor through heat</note> them like a vapor toward the surface of the earth. For the earth is porous, and that wind or vapor, trickling through the pores of the earth, is resolved into water, from which things...

# PREFACE.

->PRÆFATIO.<-

...all things are born. Likewise, when the vapor passes through places where the fatness <note>original: "pinguedo"</note> of **Sulphur** <term>The masculine, combustible, and stabilizing principle that provides form to the fluid Mercury</term> adheres, that vapor, which the **Philosophers** <note>The alchemists of old</note> called **Mercury** <term>In alchemy, Mercury represents the fluid, transformative, and feminine principle of matter</term>, adapts itself and joins with that fatness. From this mixture, a certain oiliness is created, and the name "vapor" is left behind.

## Treatise 6.
## Tract. 6.

The four **elements** <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water</note>, by perpetual motion, drip into the bowels of the earth a heavy vapor of water, which is the **seed** of metals. This is called Mercury because of its fluidity and its ability to join with every thing—not because of its actual essence. It is likened to Sulphur because of its internal heat. When it is joined to the fatness of the earth, it then becomes the **oily radical moisture** <note>original: "humidum radicale unctuoſum"; the fundamental life-fluid of a substance</note>.

## Treatise 8.
## Tract. 8.

The elements, contending among themselves, cast their seed into the center of the earth as if into the kidneys. The center, in truth, through motion sends that seed out into the **womb** <note>original: "matrix"</note>. These wombs <term>The "matrix" refers to the specific geological pocket or environment where a mineral is formed</term> are indeed innumerable; as many places as there are, so many are the wombs—one purer than another, and so on almost to infinity. Know therefore, that a pure womb will give a pure offspring <note>original: "fætum"</note> in its own likeness.

It describes the earth's interior as containing countless "wombs" that determine the purity of the resulting metal.</summary>

# PREFACE

->PREFACE<-

<meta>original Latin header: PRÆFATIO. Catchword: Æth-</meta>

*Treatise 4. If the vapor passes through warm and pure places, with which it is joined, it becomes **Gold** <note>original: "sol"; literally "the Sun," the alchemical name for gold</note>. If the places are cold and impure, it becomes **Lead** <note>original: "Saturnus"; the astrological name for lead</note>, etc.*

## ADDITION

Regarding the **first matter** <term>original: *prima materia*; the formless, primordial substance from which all things are created</term> and the generation of metals, there are almost as many different opinions as there have been **philosophers** <note>In this context, referring to alchemists and natural scientists of the Renaissance</note> who have written about them. Among these, **Theophrastus Paracelsus** <note>A Swiss physician and alchemist (1493–1541) who pioneered the use of minerals in medicine</note> has, in my opinion, shot closest to the mark. He called **minerals** the **fruits of the element of Water**, just as, by contrast, he named **plants** the **fruits of the element of Earth**.

The former [minerals] have their roots in the seawater, but they take their **primordial medium** <term>original: *Chaos*; for Paracelsus, this refers to the specific element or "atmosphere" in which a thing grows</term> within the earth and grow up within it. Plants, however, have their roots in the earth but seek their medium in the air, in which they sprout and grow tall.

Furthermore, he gives us to understand that minerals receive their masculine seed—which is their form—from heavenly influences; their **matter** <note>original: "materiam"</note> and **body**, however, they receive from the seawater. Within the belly of the earth, no differently than in a **womb** <term>original: *matrice*; the specific geological pocket or environment where a mineral is formed</term>, they are brought to maturity through the action of the internal **mountain-fire** <term>original: *Bergfeuer*; the subterranean heat believed to drive the "cooking" or ripening of metals</term>, or...

<summary>This page compares the generation of minerals to that of plants, citing Paracelsus's theory that minerals are the "fruit" of water matured within the earth's "womb" by subterranean heat.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, first matter, generation of metals, elements, mountain-fire, alchemical womb, minerals vs plants</keywords>

# PREFACE

->PREFACE<-

**Volcanic heat** <note>original: "Æthniſch Hitz"; referring to Etnean heat, the intense subterranean fire associated with volcanoes like Mount Etna</note>, which is driven upwards from the **center of the earth** <note>original: *centro terræ*</note>, can be found in varying **degrees** <note>original: *gradibus*</note>, being stronger or weaker. Likewise, the aforementioned places and **wombs** <term>original: *matrices*; the subterranean cavities where minerals were thought to "gestate" like embryos in a mother's womb</term> of the earth are qualified differently through the various **influences** of the heavens, whose **rays** <note>original: *Radiis*</note> may shoot even into the center point of the earth.

From this process—and according to how these forms are incorporated and **imprinted**—with the addition or contribution of the watery or earthy **mercurial substance**, **minerals** grow and are born in different shapes, kinds, and natures. That this is indeed the case, and that the **element of water** (which is the salty seawater, impregnated every day and night by the sun and moon, and which does not merely flow around and surround the **globe of the earth** from the outside, yet **laterally** <note>original: *lateraliter*; meaning from the sides or around the circumference</note>, but also and much more wanders internally through hidden, secret crevices and water-passages) makes the so-called **empty and void earth** <note>original: *terram vacuam & inanem*; a Latin reference to the primordial state of the world described in Genesis 1:2</note> fruitful with its salty nature; and from the heavenly influence **in this very act of conception** <note>original: *in hoc ipſo conceptionis actu*</note>, a

It describes a biological-style gestation where seawater, influenced by the sun and moon, penetrates deep into the earth to "impregnate" the void matter and initiate the growth of metals.</summary>

# PREFACE

->PREFACE<-

imprinted power or **impression** <note>original: *Impression*; refers to the alchemical concept of a celestial or spiritual "stamp" being fixed onto physical matter</note> runs along with it, it is unnecessary to explain at length here; the many types of fresh waters prove it, which initially **flow from the abyss of the sea** <note>original: *ex abysso maris*</note> into the earth, after they have now deposited their salt—whether much, little, or entirely—and the earth has drawn it to itself, they spring forth again with no or very little salty taste. Our common kitchen or table salt itself proves it, as the **center of the element of water** <note>original: *centrum Elementi aquæ*</note>, (for its **shell and surface** <note>original: *cortex & superficies*</note> is **simple, tasteless water** <note>original: *aqua insipida simplex*</note>) with which all **minerals** and **metals**—understand their **purer parts** <note>original: *puriores illorum partes*</note>—can, both without and with its appropriate additive, in many ways and manners—where one **proceeds** skillfully and not **sophistically** <term>sophistically; in alchemy, this refers to "sophistry" or deceptive, superficial practices that do not follow the true laws of nature</term>—be **radically** <note>original: *radicaliter*; meaning at the root or fundamental level</note> opened up, and then further, as if born anew in their mother (especially **gold**), they can be abundantly increased and exalted in their innate powers.

With what desire even desolate and empty earths take the nature of salt into themselves...

<meta>catchword: neh- (nehmen)</meta>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from page 11 regarding the earth's absorption of salt. It focuses on the alchemical theory that salt, rather than mercury, is the primary "principle" or "seed" from which metals are generated, citing both laboratory experience and the hidden work of the Archaeus.</meta>

### PREFACE.

...take <note>This continues the word "nehmen" (to take) from the previous page.</note>, as experience testifies, for it is found that the more often a salt water is poured or filtered through a sack full of **clay** <note>original: *Leihmen*; loam or clay soil</note> (though one clay performs better than another), the sweeter the water becomes, leaving its **salt-sharpness** <note>original: *Salzschärffe*</note> behind in the clay.

And let no one be surprised that here we have proceeded so directly and strongly upon **salt** as the foremost **principle of metallic generation** <note>original: *Principium der Metallischen Generation*</note>, while **mercury** <note>original: *Mercurius*</note>, by contrast, is seemingly skipped over.

For since every **spermatic thing** <note>original: *Spermatisch Ding*; the reproductive "seed" or essence of a substance</note> is a **salty liquor** <note>original: *Salziger Liquor*</note>, it must follow that **mercury**, which is commonly held to be the immediate principle and **seed of metals** <note>original: *Sperma metallorum*</note>, must have originated from salt—just as, through **Art** <term>Art; referring to the skill of alchemy and human chemistry</term>, a mercury may be made from any salt. If this is possible for Art, why then should it not also be possible for **Nature**? But as to how this may happen—since our eyes cannot see so far and deep down—we must entrust and defer it to the skill of the **Archaeus** <term>Archaeus; in Paracelsian philosophy, the "inner master-worker" or vital force that directs the transformation of matter</term>, as the most ingenious servant and laboratory assistant of Nature.

->Just as<-

<summary>The author argues that salt is the fundamental "seed" of all metals, claiming that even mercury is derived from it. He asserts that while human alchemy (Art) can prove this, the true process is carried out deep within the earth by the Archaeus, Nature's master chemist.</summary>

<keywords>salt, mercury, metallic generation, seed of metals, Archaeus, filtration, alchemical principles, Art vs Nature</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural generation from page 11 and 12, moving from the role of salt to the interaction between the earth's internal "vapors" and celestial influences.</meta>

->### PREFACE.<-

Just as the **interior and deeper parts of the earth** <note>original: *interiora & profundiora loca terræ*</note> are moistened and made fertile by the internal watercourses from the depths of the salty sea: so also do the Sun and Moon—through rain and dew, along with the influence of other stars—seed the upper part of the earth, or the **surface of the earth** <note>original: *superficiem terræ*</note>, from which herbs and trees, foliage and grass, then shoot forth and grow.

Just as the thicker and heavier **vapor** <note>original: *Vapor*</note> is driven up from the depths of the earth to provide the **Female Seed**, from which (with the addition and **impression** <note>original: *Impression*; here meaning a formative influence</note> of the upper **firmamental influence** <note>original: *firmamentischen Influenz*</note>, acting as the **Male Seed**) the **metals** <note>original: *metalla*</note> and **minerals** <note>original: *mineralia*</note> grow in the **deeper bowels of the earth** <note>original: *visceribus terræ profundioribus*</note>: so too is the more subtle, thinner, lighter, and more volatile **vapor**—which swings itself all the way up **to the surface of the earth** <note>original: *usque ad superficiem terræ*</note>—precisely that **fluid of the earth** <note>original: *liquor terræ*</note> and Female Seed from which (with the addition of the upper **astral influence** <note>original: *astralischen Impression*</note>, acting as the Male Seed) trees and herbs grow upon the **surface of the earth** <note>original: *superficie terræ*</note>; regarding which, let this be enough.

<summary>This page explains the alchemical theory of generation, where rising vapors from the earth act as a 'female seed' that produces minerals when trapped deep underground or plants when they reach the surface, provided they are fertilized by 'male' celestial influences.</summary>

<keywords>alchemical generation, male and female seeds, vapors, celestial influence, minerals, botany, macrocosm, firmament</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page transitions from a general discussion of natural generation (vapors and seeds) to the specific requirements for creating the Philosopher's Stone, distinguishing the alchemist's work from the natural formation of common metals.</meta>

->### PREFACE.<-

I turn myself accordingly from the **prime matter** <note>original: *materia prima*; the fundamental substance from which all things are made</note> and the **generation of metals**, to the **matter and composition of the Philosopher’s Stone** <note>original: *Lapidis Philosophici*</note>. This Stone must indeed be prepared from a metallic seed **in the place of form and Sulfur** <note>original: *loco formæ & Sulphuris*</note>, and from a moisture of the prime matter **in the place of matter and Mercury** <note>original: *loco materiæ & Mercurii*</note>. How both the one and the other part are to be understood follows briefly:

Those are mistaken who think that the matter of the **Philosopher's Stone** is the same as the first matter of metals. Indeed, the intention of Nature is one thing, and the intention of the **artist** <note>original: *artificis*; here referring to the alchemist</note> is another. It is not the artist's purpose to generate ordinary metals; therefore, he requires neither the matter nor the process which Nature uses. Rather, he strives for something more excellent, which Nature by itself never accomplishes in any place. For this reason, he has need of another **method** <note>original: *methodo*</note> and another matter. This remains true despite the fact that, in his method, he imitates **Nature** to some extent; and regarding this matter, he draws and borrows it from the same sources and principles from which Nature sought its own.

<meta>catchword: Simi-</meta>

<summary>The author distinguishes the creation of the Philosopher's Stone from the natural formation of metals, arguing that while the alchemist mimics natural principles, they require a different method and specific "seeds" of Sulfur and Mercury to achieve a result more perfect than Nature's own.</summary>
<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, prime matter, alchemical artist, Sulfur and Mercury, generation of metals, imitation of nature, alchemical method</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 14 regarding the distinction between the work of Nature and the work of the Alchemist. Having established that the artist requires a different method than Nature's ordinary metal-making process, the author now defines where Nature ends and where the "Art" of alchemy begins, focusing on the "seed" found within gold.</meta>

->### PREFACE.<-

Therefore, the **matter of the art** <note>original: *artis materia*; the substance the alchemist works with</note> may be somewhat similar to the **matter of nature**; yet they are not entirely the same and one. Moreover, nature is necessary to the **artist** <note>original: *artifici*; the alchemist</note> as a guide and author, both in the management of the matter which she supplies, and in the formation of the **Gold-making Stone** <note>original: *Lapidis aurifici*; a variation of the Philosopher's Stone specifically noted for its transmutative power</note>, which nature promotes and multiplies when aided by art. Understand it in this way: it is not for the artist to be occupied in creating the matter. For that which has been created does not need a creator. Always, the **ultimate matter** of Nature—the point where she has finished and ceased—is the **first matter** of art, the point where art begins. This is exactly the case with gold. Therefore, one must begin from it. Truly, just as in nature nothing grows without a seed, so every thing possessing a seed can be multiplied in that same seed through art, with nature assisting and supplying its growth or the substance for its increase. That a **gold-making seed** <note>original: *semen aurificum*</note> is present in gold, no one of sound mind will deny.

Wherefore, it can be multiplied in its own seed by means of art. And so, consequently, gold will be the matter or subject from which the **Seminary Form** <note>original: *Forma Seminaria*; the generative or "seeding" principle that allows the stone to grow</note> of the stone, just as

->### PREFACE.<-

the primary principle of this [stone], is to be drawn forth.

For since **Mother Nature** <note>original: *Natura parens*</note> generates and multiplies all things in two ways, namely through the male and the female, or through the active and the passive: therefore it is necessary that in this matter **Art** <note>The practice of alchemy</note> imitates Nature. And so, the active principle or **form** of this Art will be the aforementioned **seed of gold** <note>original: *semen auri*</note>, which, for its own multiplication, assimilates the matter to itself.

Truly, the role of the passive and of the matter—which multiplies and exalts the **tincture of the ferment** <note>original: *tincturam fermenti*; the alchemical agent that "leavens" or transforms base metals into gold</note>—will be performed by that **Mercury** which the **Philosophers** <note>The historic term for alchemists and natural scientists</note> called "their own." Nor does it stand in the way that the Philosophers sometimes use these names interchangeably.

For whatever it is that acts in the beginning, that same thing must be acted upon in the end, and vice-versa. Moreover, although each may be of a different sex, common Nature teaches that they must be of the same origin; through which we see every individual Being rejoice and delight in its own nature, and thereby be preserved and multiplied. For **nature overcomes nature, receives nature, and retains nature.** <note>original: *Natura enim naturam vincit, recipit ac retinet*; a foundational axiom of alchemy attributed to Pseudo-Democritus, suggesting that only substances of a similar nature can transform one another.</note>

->### PREFACE.<-

Therefore, just as the seed of gold <note>original: *semen auri*; the generative essence of the metal</note> in its first generation drew its origin from celestial influence: so too natural reason dictates that for its regeneration and multiplication, the *Philosophical Mercury* <term>Philosophical Mercury</term> <note>Not common quicksilver, but a purified, spiritualized substance used by alchemists as a universal solvent</note> must be borrowed and sought from the rays of the *Sun* <note>In alchemy, the Sun often represents gold or the active, masculine principle</note> and the *Moon* <note>The Moon represents silver or the passive, feminine principle</note> and other stars. This, therefore, will be the solvent, and the other the thing to be dissolved. This is volatile, the other fixed. From their conjunction is born for us that most noble and *Royal* infant, the *son of the Sun* <note>original: *filius Solis*; a common metaphor for the Philosopher’s Stone, representing the perfect union of spiritual and physical forces</note>.

Furthermore, because the astral influences—serving as the other, or material, principle of the stone—are all universal and spiritual, they do not fall under our senses or touch without an appropriate body. Therefore, their bodily receptacle must also exist in a universal nature or state, and be as close as possible to the excellence of the simplicity of the elements. Since, moreover, the *Element of Earth* <note>original: *Elementum terræ*</note> is of such a kind and is the general receptacle of all influences.

## CHAPTER I.
### CONCERNING THE
# SUBJECT OF EARTH,
### as the lower part of the matter of the Philosopher's Stone. <note>original: "Lapis Philosophicus"</note>

The text follows: Since the Earth is the mother for the birth of metals <term>Metallic Birth</term> <note>The alchemical belief that metals grow like embryos within the womb of the Earth.</note>, just as the Heaven is the father: It follows then, that for the preparation of the Philosopher's Stone, we must borrow <note>original: "mutuiren"</note> a piece of the matter from the aforementioned Earth. However, because many different species, kinds, and sorts of earth are found here and there: necessity requires that for the constitution of the earthly part of the named matter <term>Materia</term> <note>The raw substance or "First Matter" required to begin the alchemical transformation.</note>, a selection <note>original: "delectus"</note> must be made, and one earth must be chosen before the others for this purpose.

Among these, the naturally red clay or loam <term>Loam</term> <note>In this context, a heavy, nutrient-rich soil or clay often associated by alchemists with the "First Matter."</note> has always been found to be the best compared to others—whether they be black, white, or yellow—and because of its color, it has been called by some **Adamic Earth** <note>original: *Terra Adamica*. A reference to the belief that the biblical Adam was created from red clay.</note>, as well as **Spanish Earth** <note>original: *Hispanica*</note>.

And although the aforementioned clay is to be found everywhere, it is nevertheless encountered in one place or another as nobler and more beautiful, better or worse, and more particular or more universal. For the **Philosophers** <note>The "Philosophors" refers to the ancient and medieval authorities on alchemy.</note> have always considered that which is dug up toward the **East** <note>original: *Orient*</note>, or the rising of the sun, as well as that which is found near mines, to be the noblest and best. This earth then carries three hidden salts within itself, of which one is found to be volatile, like **sal ammoniac** <note>original: *Salmiac*; ammonium chloride, which sublimes when heated.</note>, the second of a middle nature and nitrous <note>original: *Salnitrisch*</note>, and the third entirely fixed and alkaline <note>original: *Alcalisch*</note>. After these three are mixed, the Philosophers have called them the **Body** <note>original: *Corpus*</note> and a piece of their **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurii*</note>, as well as the **Earthly Liquid Water**.

# The First Chapter.

![A woodcut illustration depicting a rugged landscape with stylized, rocky mountains under a sky with horizontal clouds. On the right side, a gnarled, leafless tree leans over the terrain. In the center foreground, a serpent or dragon-like creature lies amidst the rocks.](image-desc)

Although the highly praiseworthy Sendivogius wrote his book only for those <note>original: *qui nempe in hac laudatissimâ scientiâ prima & realia posuerunt fundamenta*; "who have indeed laid the first and real foundations in this most praiseworthy science."</note>: As he himself acknowledges and says in his **Epilogue** <note>original: *Epilogo*</note>: "I have spoken all things openly. Only the extraction of our **sal ammoniac** <term>Salis nostri armoniaci</term> <note>In alchemy, this is not common table salt but a "hidden" salt necessary for the work.</note>, or **Philosophical Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurii Philosophici*; the essential spirit or "living" essence of metals.</note> from our **pontic water** <note>original: *aquâ nostrâ ponticâ*; 'pontic' literally means 'from the sea,' but here refers to a sharp, mineral solvent used in the process.</note> I have not revealed so openly, for I have no further license to speak from the **Master of Nature** <note>original: *Magistro Naturæ*; a personification of the natural laws or the Creator.</note>, and this God alone must reveal, etc." in such a manner and so / that the **preparation** <note>original: *Praeparation*</note> of the **Philosophical Mercury**, as the true beginning and main part / is entirely omitted in his **Light** <note>original: *Lumine*; a reference to Sendivogius's famous treatise *The New Light of Alchemy* (*Novum Lumen Chymicum*).</note> / and

this is taken / set down and indicated with clear

report a little further / which is not unserviceable

And first it seems good to me / for the con-
firmation of the text of this chapter / to 
add here a **Comparison** <term>Comparation: an analogy used to explain complex concepts</term> or likeness / 
which I have read in an ancient **manuscript** <term>manuscripto: a hand-written text</term>
of similar content and **Subject** <term>Subjecto: the base matter under investigation</term>
as follows: *“Just as man, created from the earth,*
*does not generate man from the earth,*
*but from himself. However, man is nourished*
*from the earth and takes his growth from*
*thence, so that he may be able to generate:*
*Similarly, gold and silver generate*
*gold and silver. It is necessary,*

*which is, to call it by its true name,*

under which name all similar **Ochers** <term>Ochrae: natural earth pigments containing iron oxide</term> & **Lemnian earths** <term>terrae Lemniae: a medicinal clay from the island of Lemnos, often used as a substitute for Armenian Bole</term>,

Furthermore, several **boles** <term>Boli: medicinal or alchemical clays, often reddish in color, used for their astringent and healing properties</term> are encountered in the **fissures** <term>Klüften: cracks or crevices in the rock</term> and **mountain veins**, between which the **ore veins** <term>Erzgänge: the paths or layers of mineralized rock containing metals</term> run / which are also not to be despised / yet one must ensure / that they have not wandered too far from their **general nature** <note>original: *Generalität*; in alchemy, this refers to the universal, undifferentiated state of the "Subject" before it matures into a specific metal</note> / or taken on too much of the **specific nature** <note>original: *Specialische Natur*</note> of other mineral types / although such boles, which had already inclined toward a particular nature / (which is to be tested by their **vitriolic taste** <note>original: *victriolischem Sapore*; a sharp, metallic flavor indicating the presence of sulfates</note>) / can soon be helped back to their **simplicity** <note>original: *Simplicität*; the state of being a pure, unadmixed primary substance</note> by means of the described **preparation**, or better said, **reduction**. # The First Chapter.

Let this now be for you / beloved reader / a path and preparation / toward a new noble birth / which should always be regarded as a **tincture** <term>tinctur: a potent medicinal essence or alchemical substance capable of transforming other bodies</term> when compared to the first and old one.

For this reason / although **human matter** <term>Rebis humanum: literally "the human thing," often used in alchemy to refer to human excrement as a raw material</term> **preeminently** <note>original Greek: *κατ' ἐξοχὴν* (kat' exochen)</note> surpasses all other **manures** <note>original Latin: *stercora*</note>: yet one has also found through **specific experience** <note>original Latin: *in speciali experientia*</note> / that **sheep dung** (which **Sendivogius** <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist known for his writings on the "central salt" or nitre</note> briefly touches upon in his **riddle** <note>original: *ænigmate*</note> with the words: **IN THE BELLY OF THE RAM** <note>original Latin: *IN VENTRE ARIETIS*</note>) is the most convenient and suitable for this / because it is closer to and more aligned with the nature of **Saltpeter** <term>Salnitrischen: relating to nitre or saltpeter, which alchemists believed contained the "universal spirit"</term> / than the others / and **Nitre** <note>original: *Nitrum*</note>, being a **salt of a middle nature** <note>original Latin: *Sal mediæ Naturæ*</note>—neither too stable nor too volatile—is considered the best for this work.

Just as, on the other hand, **pigeon matter** <note>original Latin: *Rebis Columbinum*</note> follows the nature of **sal ammoniac** <term>Salis armoniaci: ammonium chloride, a volatile salt used to "cleanse" metals</term> / while **horse dung** <note>original Latin: *fimus equinus*</note>, etc., contains and carries within itself the nature of **alkaline salt** <note>original Latin: *Salis alcali*</note>.

Now, the **method** <note>original Latin: *modus*</note> of this **fermentation** <term>Fermentation: the alchemical process of "ripening" or increasing a substance's power through warmth and decay</term>

## CHAPTER II.
->Concerning the<-
# DISTILLATION OF THE RED
### or Adamite Earth into a liquor.
<note>The "Red" or "Adamite" earth (*Terra Rubra* or *Adamica*) is a symbolic name for the "purest" earth. Alchemists often derived this from the Hebrew name "Adam," which they interpreted as meaning "red earth" or "clay."</note>

<header>The Second Chapter.</header>

The text continues: They (the *Philosophers* <term>Philosophi: the traditional name for alchemists and seekers of natural wisdom</term>) took that same clay and made little balls out of it, as large as can be placed into a *retort* <term>Retorten: a glass or ceramic vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</term>; they filled the retort halfway, set it in a furnace, and attached a large *receiver* <term>Recipienten: a vessel designed to collect and condense the vapors driven off by heat</term>. First, they applied a gentle heat, then a stronger fire, until the retort reached a red glow; various colors soon appeared in the receiver, and the water that was driven over into the receiver amounted to about 8 or 10 *measures* <note>original German: *Maaß*; a historical unit of volume that varied by region, roughly equivalent to a quart or liter</note>. Also, whatever *volatile salt* <term>Sale volatili: a chemical salt that vaporizes easily when heated and clings to the cooler parts of the apparatus</term> had attached itself to the neck of the retort, they added that to the *liquid* <term>liquor: the condensed fluid or essence resulting from the distillation</term>.

<meta>This page continues the practical instructions for the distillation of the "Adamite Earth" described in Chapter II. While the previous page focused on the physical formation of the earth into balls for the retort, this page introduces symbolic interpretations from alchemical literature and an alternative method for concentrating the material's essence.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>The Second Chapter.</header>

The *Subject of Earth* <term>Subjectum terrae: the base material or "prime matter" being worked upon in the alchemical process</term> is called the *Ore of our Steel* <term>Minera Chalybis nostri: a symbolic term used by alchemists to hide the identity of their starting mineral; "Chalybs" usually refers to a purified or "magnetic" form of iron or mineral</term> in the *Enigma of Sendivogius* <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a famous Polish alchemist and physician whose writings, such as the *New Chemical Light*, were foundational to 17th-century alchemy.</note>. That a water must now be prepared from it, the aforementioned author intended to indicate in the said enigma through the appearance of the Water-God *Neptune* <note>In alchemical allegory, Neptune often represents the "mercurial" or liquid principle that must be extracted from the solid "earth."</note>.

->Addition.<-

It should be noted that some *Philosophers* <term>Philosophi: a term for alchemists who sought the deeper laws of nature</term> did not form any *little balls* <note>original Latin: *globulos*</note> or *wafers* <note>original Latin: *placentulas*</note>. Instead, so that they might bring the widely dispersed power into a tight concentration and obtain more from the *distilled liquor* <term>liquore stillaticio: the fluid condensed from vapor during distillation</term> through a *single distillation* <note>original Latin: *unicâ distillatione*</note> than otherwise occurs in four or five: they boiled a lye out of the aforementioned earth using common water, filtered it, and evaporated it to a *solid consistency* <note>original Latin: *consistens*</note>. Only then, once they had collected a sufficient and large amount of that same concentrated substance, did they distill it while maintaining the indicated *degrees* <note>original Latin: *graduum*</note> of fire. In this way, a wild, *fierce, untamed, and fluctuating spirit* <note>original Latin: *efferus, indomitus & fluctuans spiritus*</note>, *like the waves of the sea* <note>original Latin: *tanquam fluctus maris*</note>, passed over for them, *which finally came to rest upon its own water* <note>original Latin: *qui tandem quievit super suam aquam*</note>.

<summary>This page identifies the "Earth" as the "Ore of our Steel" and cites the alchemist Sendivogius's allegorical use of Neptune to explain the extraction of liquid. ## dead head, and its resolution

The text continues: Afterwards, they crushed the burnt pellets <note>original German: *Küglein*; small balls or spheres of the matter</note> from the first distillation into small pieces. They poured the reserved *phlegm* <term>phlegma: the watery, tasteless liquid separated during rectification, used here as a solvent</term>, or else some other pure water, over them; they filtered it and extracted the *fixed salt* <term>Salis Fixi: the stable, non-volatile mineral components that can only be recovered by washing the ashes</term>; and they added this same salt to the *spiritual water* <note>the concentrated spirit produced in the previous chapter</note>. Thus the *body* <note>Latin: *Corpus*</note>, acting as the earthly part of their *Mercury* <term>Mercurii: here referring to the "Philosophical Mercury," the fundamental substance of the alchemical Work</term>, is prepared.

<header>The Fourth Chapter.</header>

### ADDITION.

This liquid water, prepared as shown from the **Ore of Steel** <note>original Latin: *Minera Chalybis*; likely referring to an iron-based mineral used as the starting material</note>, is called **our Magnet** <note>original Latin: *Magnes noster*</note> by **Sendivogius** <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a famous Polish alchemist and physician known for his theory that a "food of life" existed in the air</note>. It is so named because it afterward draws to itself the celestial influence **of the Sun and Moon** <note>original Latin: *Solis & Lunæ*</note>, as will be taught in the following chapters. Through this influence, the water is **thickened** <note>original German: *ingrossirt*; meaning to become concentrated or more substantial</note> into a crystalline dry water, which is represented in the **riddle of Sendivogius** <note>original Latin: *ænigmate Sendivogii*</note> by old **Saturn**, the god of the earth.

At the end of the text in the first chapter, three kinds of **salts** <note>original German: *Salien*</note> are mentioned. Just as these salts rest and are hidden within the indicated **ore of our steel, or the subject of the earth** <note>original Latin: *minera Chalybis nostri vel subjecto terræ*</note>, they are likewise found in every specific thing. Each can be extracted individually, and all three can be drawn out one after another. With these salts—sometimes **together** <note>original Latin: *conjunctim*</note>, sometimes **separately** <note>original Latin: *separatim*</note>, and with each one individually—the **Philosophers** <note>the alchemists</note> have gone to work and have performed great things. Concerning this, I wish to report a little here.

# The Fourth Chapter.

<meta>catchword: Wir- (Work-)</meta>

Indeed, the **volatile salt or sal ammoniac** <note>original Latin: *Sali volatili seu armoniaco*</note>, which is not unfittingly called the **Sulfur and Salt of nature** <note>original Latin: *Sulphur & Sal naturæ*</note>, and also the **Mercury of the Philosophers** <note>original Latin: *Mercurium Philosophorum*</note>, has been greatly sought after as the more worthy and truly **Royal Salt** <note>original Latin: *Regio Sali*</note>. This same salt is raised and brought forth from **fresh earth or any subject** <note>original Latin: *ex terra recenti vel quolibet subjecto*</note> by means of distillation and the subsequent **putrefaction** <note>the process of allowing matter to rot or ferment in a gentle heat to release its essence</note>, in its own (or otherwise closely related, convenient, and **appropriate**) **liquor**; then by the soaking and **fixation** <note>the process of making a volatile substance stable enough to withstand fire</note> of that same liquor into its earth; and finally through **sublimation** <note>the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again in a purified form</note> (leaving aside here other and more numerous **ways** <note>original Latin: *modorum*</note> to make this salt). This they afterward **waxed** <note>original German: *incerirt*; the process of softening a substance to a wax-like consistency</note> and **fixed** <note>original German: *figirt*</note> **with the oil of the Sun toward the Stone** <note>original Latin: *cum oleo Solis ad Lapidem*; referring to the final stages of creating the Philosopher's Stone using a gold-based essence</note>. Some have dissolved it with its own spiritual water, or even driven it over as a volatile substance for the **dissolution** of the **two Luminaries** <note>the "Luminaries" are the Sun and Moon, representing gold and silver</note>. 

But **saltpeter** <note>original Latin: *Salnitrum*</note>, when artfully and well purified, is in no way inferior to the aforementioned **sal ammoniac**, not only because of its easy preparation—which requires no special skill and is a **simple woman’s work** <note>original German: *einfältige Weiber Arbeit*; a common alchemical trope referring to a process that is easy and requires only patience and steady, gentle heat, much like laundry or cooking</note>—but also because of its power and **work-** <note>the text cuts off at the catchword *Wir-*, likely for *Wirckung*, meaning effects or operations</note>

# The Fourth Chapter.

...effect, by which it far surpasses that same thing, after it has been properly brought to its end **according to the rules of the art** <note>original Latin: *lege artis*; meaning following the established procedures of alchemy</note>. This is now brought about without distillation, only **through a simple overflow** <note>original Latin: *per simplicem exuberationem*; referring to the natural formation of salt crystals on the surface of earth</note> by means of basic leaching, as is common and well-known to the **saltpeter boilers** <note>original German: *SalpeterSiedern*; tradesmen who extracted saltpeter from soil found in stables and cellars</note>. 

If you now know how to find the right earth (**Since one is more desirable than the other** <note>original Latin: *Siquidem una est eligibilior alterâ*</note>, of which sufficient indication was given in the **preceding sections** <note>original Latin: *præcedentibus*</note>), then it is very good. If you can then rightly and well purify the leached **saltpeter** <note>original Latin: *nitrum*</note>, it is better still. 

But if you can **strengthen** <note>original Latin: *fortificiren*</note> its qualities in the **very act of purification** <note>original Latin: *ipso clarificationis actu*</note>, whether it be done with heavenly or earthly powers, **or something of the sort** <note>original Latin: *vel quasi*</note>, then it is best of all. You have good reason to thank God for this great knowledge, on account of this very easy and minor labor, for by this you are relieved of much difficult and dangerous work. You need not inquire after the other two **salts** <note>original Latin: *Salibus*</note>, whether they be **fixed** <note>stable in fire</note> or volatile, nor after the **astral impregnation** <note>the belief that celestial influences "fertilize" the earth to create minerals</note> or **impression**, since this salt contains enough of everything within itself that one requires, **except for the ferment** <note>original Latin: *excepto fermento*; the specific substance added at the end of the process to "seed" the transformation into gold or silver</note>.

...golden <note>original: *aureo*</note>, with which it must be animated and then fully boiled out. Afterward, both **oils** <note>original: *Olitäten*; refers here to the essential fatty or oily essences extracted from the salt and the gold</note>—that of the aforementioned salt and that of the gold—are joined together and **coagulated into the Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note>, achieving a great **Projection** <note>The final stage of alchemy where the Stone is cast upon base metals to transform them into gold</note>. This should also be understood regarding the **fixed salt of our mineral** <note>original: *fixo Sale nostræ mineræ*</note>, of which the text of this chapter speaks. 

But he who does not have a **golden hook to fish with** <note>A metaphorical expression meaning one who lacks the initial gold or the financial means to start the expensive gold-based process</note> should take, instead of gold, **common mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium vulgi*</note>, **precipitated and fixed by itself and in itself alone** <note>original: *per se & in se solo præcipitatum & fixum*</note>. With this, I wish to conclude this chapter.

The Sixth Chapter.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut divided into two panels. The left panel depicts a sun and a crescent moon, both with faces, set against a sky filled with stars. Below, on a patch of earth, sits a wooden tub from which a winged or leaf-like form emerges. The right panel shows a similar wooden tub on the ground, with a large, stylized cloud or celestial wing-like shape in the sky above it.</image-desc>

Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a renowned Polish alchemist and physician, famous for his theories on the "food of life" found in the air.</note> in the epilogue: Consider diligently how the four elements drip into the center of the earth, into the radical moisture <term>Radical moisture: original *humidum radicale*. In alchemy and medieval medicine, this was the fundamental, life-sustaining fluid thought to be inherent in all matter.</term>, and how the central terrestrial Sun, by its own motion, produces and sublimes <note>original: *sublimet*. Sublimation is the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here metaphorically for refinement.</note> it to the surface of the earth. Consider also the correspondence of the celestial Sun with the central Sun. For the celestial Sun and Moon have a peculiar force and power of dripping through their rays into the earth: For heat is easily joined to heat, and Salt to salt <note>original: *Calor enim calori, Sal sali facilè jungitur.* This illustrates the Hermetic law of attraction, where like substances are drawn to one another.</note>. And just as the central Sun has its own sea and thick, crude, perceptible water: So the celestial Sun also has its own sea—a subtle and imperceptible water. Upon the surface of the earth, rays are joined to rays, and they produce flow-

<meta>catchword: -ers (from Latin *flores*)</meta>

<summary>This page features a quote from Sendivogius explaining the alchemical theory of "as above, so below." It describes how the terrestrial Sun within the earth and the celestial Sun in the heavens work in harmony to circulate moisture and vital forces.</summary>

...things, and all. Therefore when rain falls, it receives from the air that **force of life** <term>Force of life: original *vim vitæ*. Alchemists believed the air was charged with a vital, celestial spirit that could be captured.</term>, and joins it with the **salt-nitre** of the earth (because the salt-nitre of the earth is like **calcined tartar** <term>Calcined tartar: original *calcinati tartari*. A substance produced by burning the sediment of wine; it is highly hygroscopic, meaning it naturally pulls moisture out of the air.</term>, drawing the air to itself by its own dryness; this air is then resolved within it into water. The salt-nitre of the earth possesses such a power of attraction because it was once air itself, and it is joined to the **fatness of the earth** <note>original: *pinguedini terræ*. This refers to the fertile, oily substance alchemists believed was the fundamental nutrient in soil.</note>.) And the more abundantly the solar rays strike at that time, the greater the quantity of salt-nitre is produced, and consequently a greater abundance of **grain** <note>original: *frumenti*. The author argues that the growth of crops is a natural alchemical process of the earth capturing celestial life-force.</note> grows; and this happens from day to day; see more on this in the following chapter.

### ADDITION.

1. Of the **liquid salt-water** and the **captured air-water**, the weight of one should be equal to the weight of the other.
2. The glass vessel should be wide and low.
3. The glass should have a mark upon it, by which one may perceive how much of the air-water <note>original: *Lufftwasser*. Likely referring to dew or rainwater collected for the experiment.</note> has evaporated or dried up through the **warmth of the sun** <note>original: *Sonnen Wärme*.</note>, so that the loss may be restored by pouring in fresh air-water.

# The Sixth Chapter.

4. In the event that the sun shines too hotly, one should cover the top with green brushwood <note>original: *grün Reisig*. Freshly cut branches with leaves were used to provide dappled shade and prevent the mixture from overheating or evaporating too quickly.</note>.

5. It should also be placed under the **Full Moon** <note>original: *Plenilunio*. In alchemical practice, the moon was thought to provide a "cool" celestial influence that balanced the "hot" influence of the sun.</note>.

6. The longer one lets it stand **under the open sky** <note>original: *sub dio*. Alchemists believed that exposure to the night air and dew allowed the substance to absorb a "universal spirit" from the heavens.</note>, the better it is.

7. One will already notice after the first three days that it begins to **thicken** <term>Thicken: original *inspissiren*. This refers to the liquid becoming more viscous or concentrated as it prepares to crystallize.</term> and prepare for **solidification** <term>Solidification: original *congelation*. In this context, it refers to the process of crystals forming out of a liquid solution, rather than freezing due to cold.</term>.

## The seventh figure follows.

Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a famous Polish alchemist and physician who claimed to have discovered the secret of the "central salt."</note>, Treatise 10: Our **celestial water** <note>original: *Aqua nostra cœlestis*</note> that **does not wet the hands** <term>Does not wet the hands: A common alchemical description for "Philosophical Mercury," a substance that behaves like a liquid but is not ordinary water.</term> is not of the common sort, but is truly of rain. It is also called **our Moon** <note>original: *Luna nostra*</note> and the **womb** <note>original: *matrix*</note>, which receives the **seed of gold** <note>original: *semen auri*</note> (as will be made clear in what follows).

From which principles this **dry water** <note>original: *aqua sicca*</note> is prepared, Sendivogius wished to outline in brief words in Treatise 11: "Consider, I pray you, the simple **water of the clouds** <note>original: *nubis aquam*</note>! Would anyone ever believe that it contains within itself everything that the world holds—hard stones, salts, air, earth, and fire—even though it appears to be simple water on its own?"

# The Seventh Chapter.

Then they place it in a cold place or a cool cellar, so that it begins to **crystallize**. These [crystals] are taken out with a wooden ladle or tongs, and fresh **air-water** <note>This refers to water collected from the atmosphere, such as rain or dew, which alchemists believed contained a celestial spirit.</note> is poured into the remaining water. It is then set out again **under the open sky** <note>original: *sub dio*</note> as before, and this is repeated as long as crystals continue to grow.

In this regard, I did not want to omit mentioning that some **philosophers** <note>In this context, "philosophers" refers to practitioners of alchemy.</note> have mixed these crystals with the **raw prime matter** <note>original: *materia prima cruda*</note>, formed balls from them, and distilled them according to the method in the second chapter. In this way, the **terrestrial spirits** came over dark, but the **celestial spirits** came over **blood-red**. They also called this water the **double Mercury of the Philosophers** <note>original: *Mercurium Philosophorum duplicem*</note>, though in a **moist and liquid form** <note>original: *formâ humidâ & liquidâ*</note>; likewise, they called it **Virgin’s Milk** <note>original: *Lac Virginis*; a common term for a white or powerful alchemical solvent</note> and the **sharpest vinegar** <note>original: *acetum acerrimum*</note>.

With this **solvent** <term>Menstruo: Derived from the Latin *menstruum*, this refers to a powerful solvent used to dissolve solids and extract their essence.</term> <note>original: *Menstruo*</note>, they dissolved all metals freely "upon the table" <note>Meaning the reaction occurred easily at room temperature without the need for a furnace.</note> and were able to extract their **sulfur** <note>original: *Sulphur*</note> and **souls** <note>original: *animas*</note>. (For it causes the **physical bodies** <note>original: *Corpora*</note> to precipitate to the bottom). However, one cannot dissolve **gold** with it unless the **fixed salt** <note>original: *Sal fixum*</note> from that same distillation has also been dissolved in such a **liquid** <note>original: *liquore*</note> beforehand

# The Eighth Chapter.

**In rivers or in mountain ores** <note>original: *in fluminibus aut montium mineris*</note>, whoever can obtain this, stays most securely by the literal meaning of the text. However, **sometimes in this case** <note>original: *quandoque in casu*</note> the place and stead of that same **natural compact gold** <note>original: *naturalis compacti auri*</note> may be represented by the extracted **Sulfur** <note>original: *Sulphur*; the "soul" or active combustible principle in alchemy</note> from golden **ores** <note>original: *mineris*</note> and **marcasites** <note>a term often used for iron pyrites or other metallic sulfides</note>, as well as by the gold-sand from the washing-works—provided that you find them certainly and properly influenced by the **star of the Sun** <note>original: *astro Solis*; the celestial or astrological influence believed to mature gold in the earth</note> and that they carry no other types of metal within them except gold, regardless of how little of it is found in the assay. This is because in such things a **nearness to the nature of gold** <note>original: *aureitas propinqua*</note> exists and is manifest **by the act itself** <note>original: *ipso actu*; meaning it is already gold in essence, even if in small quantities</note>. 

On the other hand, in other **metallic subjects** <note>metals or minerals being studied</note> (to which, because of their imperfection compared to the highest solar **perfection** <note>the state of being fully "ripe" or pure gold</note>, some **philosophers** <note>alchemists</note> have also given the name of **green and living gold** <note>original: *auri viridis ac vivi*</note>), the mentioned golden nature is found only in a **certain remote or even more distant potential** <note>original: *potentiâ quadam remotâ ac remotiori*</note>. This nature can be brought to the height—**that is, to the perfect state and golden degree** <note>original: *hoc est, ad perfectum statum & gradum aureum*</note>—partly through the way and mode of **progression**, and partly through **regression**, though not without special effort and labor.

It distinguishes between gold that is already "actual" and other metals where a golden nature exists only as a "remote potential" requiring great labor to perfect.</summary>

# The Eighth Chapter.

Therefore, it is not without reason that **Paracelsus** <note>The famous Swiss physician and alchemist (1493–1541) known for his radical approach to medicine and chemistry</note> rejects such a **remote potential for gold-ness** <note>original: *remotam potentiam aureitatis*; the theoretical possibility that any base metal could eventually become gold</note>. Instead, he looks and aims for the most closely related, actual golden growth, type, and nature, where he writes in the **first treatise On Minerals** <note>original: *tractatu primo de Mineralibus*</note>: If the **Alchemists** could find this **Sulfur** <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, masculine, and combustible "soul" of a substance</note>, as it is indeed found in the **Gold-Tree** and its roots, they would have reason to rejoice. For that is the **Sulfur of the Philosophers** <note>original: *Sulphur Philofophorum*; a refined version of the sulfur principle specifically derived from gold</note> from which gold is made—not the other type, from which iron, copper, etc., are made. This is the **critical point** <note>original: *Scrupel*; here used to mean a fundamental difficulty or a "stumbling block"</note> of their **universal medicine** <note>original: *universals*; referring to the "Universal" or the Philosopher's Stone that can cure all or transmute all</note>. **These are his words** <note>original: *Hæc ille*</note>.

Although the text of this chapter is grandly **confirmed** by this authority, it has nonetheless pleased me to set down here a simile for further confirmation, which the aforementioned Paracelsus introduces regarding this matter. He writes in the **second treatise On the Method of Preparing Medicines** <note>original: *in secundo Tractatu de modo pharmacandi*</note> as follows: Note the difference between the stones in water and those outside of water. For the one in the water exists in its **living liquids** <note>original: *vividis liquoribus*; the vital, moist essences believed to nourish and "grow" minerals within the earth</note>, whereas the one outside is in the loss of the same. Herein lies, says **Paracelsus**, the error of the **Alchemists**.

<summary>The author cites Paracelsus to argue that true gold-making requires "living" sulfur found in gold's own natural roots rather than base metals. He uses the analogy of stones in water to explain that minerals lose their vital, transformative power once they are removed from their natural, moist environment.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, Sulfur of the Philosophers, Gold-Tree, universal medicine, living liquids, alchemical theory, mineral growth</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Paracelsus's theories from page 56. Having established that alchemists must seek the "Sulfur of the Philosophers" in living sources rather than base metals, the text now addresses the common failure to distinguish between "living" and "dead" matter in the pursuit of the Great Work.</meta>

# The Eighth Chapter.

...and those who seek to prepare the **quintessence** <term>Quintam essentiam: the "fifth element" or the purest, most concentrated essence of a natural substance</term>, great **elixirs**, great **magisteria** <term>Magisteria: masterful alchemical preparations or "masterworks"</term>, and **arcana** <term>Arcana: deep, secret medicinal or alchemical mysteries</term>. They fundamentally fail because they cannot distinguish between the **living** and the **dead**—which is also to be understood regarding melted or refined metals versus raw, unrefined ones. With this, **Sendivogius** <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a famous Polish alchemist and physician known for his theories on the "food of life" in the air.</note> agrees when he says in his seventh treatise: **In severed trees, the seed is sought in vain, for it is found only in green, living ones** <note>original: *In arboribus amputatis frustra semen quaeritur, quod solumodo in viridibus reperitur.*</note>.

And in the same place: **The seed is not found in dead things. As long as the seed remains in the body, the body lives. When it is entirely consumed, the body dies. Just as we see all bodies weakened after the emission of seed—take for example men who indulge too much in Venus** <note>original: *Semen non reperitur in rebus mortuis. Quandiu in corpore semen est, vivit corpus. Quando totum consumitur, corpus moritur. Sicut omnia corpora post emissionem seminis videmus debilitari, exemplo hominum Veneri nimis indulgentium.* In alchemical writing, "Venus" is a standard metaphor for sexual desire or the reproductive act.</note>.

In the third treatise: **Do not seek in common metals that specific point, for it is not in them. For these metals, especially the common gold, are dead; but our gold is living and possesses the Spirit, which is what must be used. Know that the life of metals is fire, while they are still in their ores; and their death is also fire—namely, the fire of smelting.** <note>original: *Ne quaeras in metallis vulgi illud punctum, in quibus non est. Nam haec metalla, praesertim aurū vulgi, mortua sunt, sed nostra viva, Spir. habentia, quae omnino sumenda sunt. Scito e. metallorum vitam esse ignem, dum adhuc in suis mineris sunt: Et mortem etiam ignem, fusionis videlicet.*</note>

Likewise, in his riddles: **The "Mars" of things is nothing other than the separation of one thing from another.** <note>original: *Mars rerum nil aliud est, quam separatio unius ab altero.* In this context, "Mars" (the god of war) represents the forceful, energetic process of dividing or purifying substances.</note>

It cites Michael Sendivogius to reinforce the distinction between raw ores (which contain life-fire) and processed metals (which have been killed by the fire of the forge).</summary>

# The Eighth Chapter.

...therefore, it is not without reason that **Paracelsus** <term>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine</term> rejects such **remote potential for goldness** <note>original: *remotam potentiam aureitatis*; this refers to the theoretical possibility of turning any substance into gold, which Paracelsus deemed too impractical or distant for the true work</note> and instead looks to and follows the most immediate, actual growth, kind, and nature of gold, where he writes in his **first treatise on Minerals** <note>original: *tractatu primo de Mineralibus*</note>: If the alchemists could find this **Sulfur** <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle of a substance, believed to give metals their color and solidity.</note>, as it is indeed easily found in the **Gold Tree** <note>The "Gold Tree" or *Arbor Philosophica* is a metaphorical image for the growth and maturation of metals within the earth.</note> and its roots, they would have cause for great joy; for that is the **Sulfur of the Philosophers** <note>original: *Sulphur Philosophorum*</note> from which gold is made—not that other sulfur which comes from iron, copper, etc. This, then, is the **difficulty** <term>Scrupel: here meaning a fundamental obstacle or a point of doubt</term> regarding their **universal medicine** <term>Universals: the *Universal* or the "Great Work," intended to create a substance that could cure all illness and transmute all metals.</term>

**Thus says he** <note>original: *Hæc iste*</note>. Although the text of this chapter is grandly confirmed by such authority, for its further corroboration I have been pleased to include here the comparison which the aforementioned **Paracelsus** introduces regarding this matter, writing as follows in his **second treatise on the method of preparing medicines** <note>original: *in secundo Tractatu de modo pharmacandi*</note>: Note the difference between stones in water and those outside of water: For the one in the water stands in its **living fluids** <note>original: *vividis liquoribus*</note>, while the one outside is in the loss of the same. In this, says **Paracelsus**, lies the error of the alchemists,

It emphasizes the distinction between living, hydrated minerals and dead, dry ones as the key to avoiding alchemical error.</summary>

# The Eighth Chapter.

...and of those who seek the **fifth essence** <term>Quinta essentia: the "quintessence" or purest, most concentrated medicinal or spiritual core of a substance</term>, and who wish to prepare great **elixirs** <term>Elixiria: liquid medicinal preparations believed to prolong life or transform metals</term>, great **magisteries** <term>Magisteria: highly refined alchemical substances produced through a specific master process</term>, and **secrets** <term>Arcana: powerful, hidden medicinal virtues or remedies</term>, yet fundamentally lack the ability to distinguish between the living and the dead—which is also to be understood regarding melted or processed metals, versus raw, unmelted **metals** <note>original: *Metalln*</note>. With this, **Sendivogius** <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a famous Polish alchemist and physician who claimed that a "vital food" for life was hidden in the air.</note> agrees, where he speaks in his **seventh treatise**:

> One seeks the seed in cut-down trees in vain, for it is found only in those that are green and living. <note>original: *In arboribus amputatis frustra semen quæritur, quod solummodo in viridibus reperitur.*</note>
> 
> In the same place: The seed is not found in dead things. As long as the seed is in the body, the body lives. When it is entirely consumed, the body dies. Just as we see all bodies weakened after the emission of the seed, following the example of men who indulge too much in **Venus** <note>original: *Veneri*; a common alchemical and early modern reference to sexual intercourse or lust.</note>. **Treatise 3**. Do not seek that vital point in the common metals, for it is not in them. For these metals, especially **common gold** <note>original: *aurũ vulgi*; the ordinary gold used by jewelers or merchants, which alchemists considered "dead" because it had been subjected to the harsh heat of the furnace.</note>, are dead; but ours are living, possessing **spirit** <note>original: *Spir.* or *spiritus*</note>, which must certainly be used. Know, therefore, that the life of metals is fire, while they are still in their ores; and their death is also fire—namely, the fire of **smelting** <term>fusionis: the melting of ore to extract metal, which Sendivogius argues "kills" the mineral's inner life.</term> **The same author in his Riddle**: <note>original: *Idem in ænigm.*; referring to his famous alchemical parables.</note> The death of things is nothing other than the separation of one from the other.

<summary>This page argues that alchemists fail when they use "dead" processed metals instead of "living" ores. Using metaphors of biology and trees, it cites Sendivogius to explain that the vital spirit or "seed" of a metal is destroyed by the heat of the smelting furnace.</summary>

# Chapter Eight.

**Paracelsus** says: An **ore** is endowed with far more virtues than its **metal**, because during smelting the **liquors of life** <note>original: *liquores vitae*</note>—namely the **spirits of salt, sulfur, mercury, and arsenic**—depart from it, leaving the metal lying dead.

Be that as it may, if one ponders these matters somewhat more deeply and thoroughly, a **philosopher** <note>In this context, "philosopher" refers to an alchemist or a student of nature.</note> is not strictly bound to the aforementioned things—which are quite difficult and not easily obtained everywhere—especially since every deceased thing may easily attain rebirth and new life in and through its **mother** <note>In alchemical thought, the "mother" is the earth or the original matrix from which minerals grow.</note>. Thus, common smelted gold and silver—which are indeed to be considered dead and barren—may be awakened and made living again with the help of **radical** <note>Meaning fundamental, or "at the root."</note> <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to a high temperature to reduce it to a fine powder</term> and <term>solution: the process of dissolving a solid substance into a liquid</term>, so that they prove themselves to be very powerful and effective both as **medicine** <note>original: *Arzney*</note> and in other matters. For if the dead **body of gold** <term>Corpus auri: the physical, metallic form of gold</term> is buried in the earth toward the East (following **Paracelsian tradition**), it will once again grow and increase in substance and weight...

<meta>The catchword "gebracht" (brought) suggests the next page will describe how the gold is "brought" to a state of growth or increase.</meta>

<summary>This page cites Paracelsus to explain that while smelting kills the "life" in metals, an alchemist can restore this vitality through calcination and solution. It suggests that even "dead" gold can be reborn and made to grow if treated according to specific traditional methods.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, metallurgy, ore vs. metal, liquors of life, spirits of salt, sulfur, mercury, arsenic, calcination, solution, medicinal gold, corpus auri</keywords>
