<meta>catchword: This</meta>

...[natu]ral, which by the benefit of **Vulcan** <note>original: *Vulcani*; in alchemy, Vulcan refers to the personification of fire or the furnace</note> alone is brought forth in a very short space of time without the addition of any extraneous thing; it receives all the qualities of the metal with which it is mixed by the industry of the wise craftsman—its smell, color, and taste, along with the preservation of its vegetative faculty. With this dissolved metal, that smoky, watery, and scorching spirit <note>original: *spiritus fumosus, aquosus, & adustivus*</note> is changed into a most noble body, and no longer flees from the fire, but flows like a fixed oil <note>original: *oleum fixum*; an oil that has been made stable so that it does not evaporate when heated</note>, and as it is transformed, it transforms [others], etc. 

Yet the ancient *philosophers* <note>original: *Philosophi*</note> looked more toward the reduction and resolution <note>the process of breaking a solid down into its constituent parts</note> of the already grown and *compact flint* <note>original: *compacti silicis*</note> into its *prime matter* <note>original: *materiam primam*</note> or salt-essence <gloss>Saltzwesen</gloss>, rather than taking such a matter from which the stone might first grow. Therefore, they calcined <note>reduced to a powder through heat</note> it *in a glowing fire* <note>original: *in igne candente*</note> and then melted it with its *companion or mixture* (which is *salt of tartar* <term>salt of tartar<gloss>sal tartari</gloss>; potassium carbonate, often used as a flux to help minerals melt at lower temperatures</term>) into a glass or crystal. 

This crystal, when prepared into an oil or *liquid* <note>original: *liquor*</note>, is a medicine <gloss>Artzney</gloss> for all tartaric diseases <note>diseases characterized by "tartar" or stony deposits in the body, such as kidney stones, gallstones, or gout</note>, and is the right and true *Ludus* or *Stone-breaker of Paracelsus* <note>original: *Saxifragus Paracelsi*; a famous alchemical remedy intended to dissolve stones within the human body</note>.

Also: This **crystal of flint**, [melted] for 5 hours with the **metal of Venus** <note>original: *metall Veneris*; copper</note>...

...just as common burnt <term>lime</term> <note>original: *Kalcks*; calcium oxide</note> otherwise allows itself to be consumed by the air, and on the other hand, draws in and incorporates the qualities of the cold air; so this crumbled <term>lime</term> has the power to <term>congeal</term> <note>original: *congeliren*; to solidify a liquid into a solid or semi-solid state</note> and <term>constrain</term> <note>original: *constringiren*; to bind or tighten the molecular structure</note> the <term>mercuries of bodies</term> <note>original: *Mercurios corporum*; the metallic essence or "quicksilver" extracted from solid metals</note>, especially that of <term>Saturn</term> <note>original: *Saturni*; the alchemical name for lead</note>. The method is as follows: Fill a <term>cementing box</term> <note>original: *Cementbüchsen*; a ceramic vessel used for "cementation," a process where a metal is heated while surrounded by a powdered reagent</note> half full with this <term>lime</term>, pressed firmly down; make a hollow in the middle; place the metallic <term>mercury</term> or <term>mercuries</term> therein; cover it completely with the same <term>lime</term> until the box is entirely filled; <term>seal it with lute</term> <note>original: *verlutirs*; to seal a vessel airtight using a paste made of clay, dung, or sand</note>; and keep it <term>in a brooding fire</term> <note>original: *in igne incubante*; a steady, gentle heat, comparable to a bird sitting on eggs</note> for twelve days; then you will find the <term>hardened mercury</term> <note>original: *Mercurium induratum*</note>. This congealed substance is fluid like resin when heated, but in the cold, it solidifies in the form of <term>bismuth</term> <note>original: *Wißmuts*</note> or <term>zinc</term> <note>original: *Zincken*</note>; and it has <term>magnetically</term> <note>original: *magneticè*</note> drawn into itself the coldness of the air which had settled into the <term>lime</term>. From this, the hot, flowing <term>mercury</term> has become freezing and solidifying, and has also been made somewhat more capable of receiving and retaining hot, fiery spirits. Especially, however, this coldness of the air is of a <term>middle nature</term> <note>original: *Mittel-Natur*; a substance that acts as a bridge between spiritual and material realms</note>, which unites the essential...

<summary>This page describes an alchemical process where lime is used to solidify mercury (specifically lead-based mercury) by utilizing the "cold qualities" of air absorbed by the lime. ...commended <note>original: *mendârunt*, likely completing *commendârunt* from the previous page</note> it among the ancients, so much so that they easily preferred it to all other medicines; for by its power, however much the strengths might languish, they are restored to such an extent that those using it seem to grow young again and appear to receive from it an incredible vigor of all the body's faculties. § (77.) §

...having been drawn together <note>original: *contracta*</note>, it is not hidden from me—indeed, I am well aware—that you, although most busy with both the zeal of writing and other cares, nevertheless devote whatever "stolen hours" you have mostly to the preparation of these rarer medicines; and that you are so moved by these things that you do not shrink from any labor that must be completed even with your own hands. Furthermore, you know the various methods devised by different men by which they have attempted to reduce gold into a drinkable liquor. For there are those who dissolve it into liquid by means of certain waters which, because of their violent corrosion, they call **strong waters** <note>original: *aquas... fortes*; known today as Nitric Acid or Aqua Fortis</note>. These are commonly prepared by **chemists** <term>chymicis</term> from one part of **saltpeter** <term>salis nitri</term> <note>Potassium nitrate</note> and two parts of **vitriol** <term>Chalcanthi</term> <note>A sulfate mineral, often copper or iron sulfate, essential for producing mineral acids</note>. By these, gold is separated from silver, and for that reason they call them **parting waters** <term>separatrices aquas</term>; for silver is dissolved into the liquid, while the gold settles to the bottom as a fine [black] powder...

If anyone adds to the previous ingredients a fourth part of **sal ammoniac** <term>salis Armoniaci</term> <note>ammonium chloride; used in alchemy to create powerful solvents</note> and prepares it as before, he will have the water which goldsmiths and **chemists** <term>Chymici</term> call **royal water** <term>aqua regia</term> <note>A mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids, so named because it is the only acid that can dissolve "royal" metals like gold</note>. In this process, the silver will settle out as it is separated, appearing like lime <note>original: *calci simile*; a white, powdery residue</note>, but the gold truly liquefies into a water of a most brilliant, shining golden color. 

But one must take extreme care that no one consumes this: for it would eat away all the internal organs. **Philippus Ulstadius** <note>A 16th-century physician and author of *Coelum Philosophorum*, a famous book on distillation and medicinal chemistry</note> provides a description of **potable gold** <term>auri potabilis</term>, which he said **Johannes of Toledo** and **Hugo**—both Cardinals—used in all their dishes and held as a most great treasure. 

However, in the water in which it is dissolved, not only **saltpeter** <term>sal nitri</term> and **sal ammoniac** are consumed into the composition, but also **sublimated mercury** <term>Mercurius sublimatus</term> <note>mercury(II) chloride, a highly toxic and corrosive salt</note>; since these are extremely corrosive and not without poison, I would advise that they should in no way be used. And not only for that reason, but it is also to be feared lest the stomach become coated with gold. For that water stains everything it touches, according to the variety of things with which it is mixed: it turns a golden color if mixed with gold, a black color if with silver or **quicksilver** <term>Mercurio vivo</term>, green if with copper, and so on. Indeed, whoever the author of it may be, he devised a method that is not foolish for removing its harmfulness—namely, that which [distills] <note>The catchword *destil-* suggests the author is describing a redistillation process to purify the solution</note> that corrosive water again...

...he hopes he will draw it off by distilling from the dissolved gold <term>auro soluto</term>, so that the gold remains at the bottom like **butter** <note>original: *butyro*; in alchemy, a "butter" refers to a thick, oily substance, often a chloride of a metal</note>. I would rather attempt to pour hot distilled water over the material and wash it repeatedly, by which undoubtedly that saltiness, and perhaps the poisonous force, would be more quickly removed. Otherwise, it is clear that the use of this supposed medicine, prepared with such great poisons, must not be rashly attempted or allowed. 

I have known others—and I possess their method of preparation—who hope they can forge **potable gold** <term>aurum potabile</term> <note>A legendary alchemical medicine consisting of gold dissolved in a liquid form, believed to grant eternal youth or cure all diseases</note> from the front part of a **human skull** <term>cranii humani</term> which has been **calcined** <term>calcinata</term> <note>reduced to powder through extreme heat</note> and reduced to a crystalline and transparent **salt** <term>sal</term>, which I think you have also seen; but whether they have actually finished it, I do not know. However this matter may stand, it is entirely necessary that a salt be prepared from the skull. Since, however, an abundance of natural salts is available to us, and equally from...

(80.)

...since salt <term>sal</term> can be produced from almost any other burnt thing, I wonder what specifically motivated them to investigate the **skull** <term>cranij</term>. I do indeed recall a saying of **Albertus Magnus** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280) was a famous medieval scholar and friar interested in the natural sciences and alchemy</note> in his work *On Minerals*, where he asserts that a great mineral virtue exists in **human hair**, especially that which is cut from the head; he also notes that in his own time, a human skull was seen and found which had many parts of **gold dust** <note>original: *aurei pulveris*</note> mixed between the "teeth" of the upper **sutures** <note>the zig-zagging joints where the bones of the skull meet</note>. I also know that **Geber** <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in early chemistry and alchemy</note> [who almost alone handed down **chemistry** <term>chymiam</term> based on the very principles of nature, and whom students of that art admire as if he were their own **Aristarchus** <note>Aristarchus was a famous Greek grammarian known for his rigorous criticism; here, it implies Geber is the ultimate authority whose word is law</note>) wonderfully recommends a certain **oil made from human hair** for **inceration** <term>inceratione</term> <note>the alchemical process of softening a dry substance until it has a wax-like consistency</note>, or as a preparation for the easier **melting of metals**. 

But even if it is granted that they achieve something by this, nevertheless this method of preparation seems very remote and far-fetched, since many other things can be found which perform that very same task by themselves, whereas the skull can hardly be brought to that point even after a long time. And this is all the more true because, in making that salt—as those who have tried to prepare it have experienced—the skull, while it is being extracted, gives off a foul and most **stinking odor** [just as most bones are accustomed <note>original: *so-* likely completing to *solent* (are accustomed)</note> to do when burning...

<meta>catchword: lent</meta>

<summary>The author expresses skepticism regarding the use of human skulls in alchemy, arguing that while figures like Albertus Magnus and Geber noted the presence of minerals in hair and bone, the process is inefficient and produces a repulsive stench compared to other methods.</summary>

...[smell] so strongly that it strikes the nostrils and infects all the surrounding air. Furthermore, I possess the method of a certain **Physician from Strasbourg** <note>original: *Argentinensis*</note> for making **potable gold** <term>aurum potabile</term> from gold alone, without any other substance added except for the power of fire. According to the rules of the **chemical art** <term>chymicæ</term>, one must take gold purified by **antimony** <term>stybium</term> <note>Antimony was commonly used in the Renaissance to refine gold, as it would "eat" the impurities, leaving the pure metal behind</note> and beaten into the thinnest sheets. These are hung freely in vessels suitable for this purpose, so that the sheets never touch one another. While the metal is being burned and **calcined** <term>calcinatur</term> <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its physical state, often reducing it to a powder or "calx"</note>, it must endure the most intense heat for half a year in a furnace properly prepared for this task. Finally, those golden sheets are removed from the heat and exposed to the open and cooler air; then, a certain oily liquid flows down from them and dissolves into a red color with a slightly sweet taste.

From one pound of gold, he would extract up to two ounces of this oil. If, however—perhaps because the gold was not equally burned on all sides by the fire—that liquid did not flow out readily enough, he used to pour in **rectified spirit of wine** <term>spiritum vini</term> <note>highly distilled, pure alcohol</note> several times. He would repeat this so long and so often until all that glowing heat <note>implying the "soul" or color of the metal</note> was extracted. A somewhat white gold powder was left at the bottom, and the spirit of wine would no longer take any more color from the gold. How very subtly indeed these spirits, or the **fifth essence** <term>quintam essentiam</term> <note>the *quinta essentia* or quintessence was believed to be the purest, most potent medicinal core of a substance, beyond the four physical elements</note>...

<summary>The author describes a lengthy, six-month alchemical process for extracting a medicinal "oil of gold" using only heat, air, and alcohol, avoiding the harsh acids criticized previously. This method, attributed to a Strasbourg physician, claims to separate the red "essence" of the gold from its white physical body.</summary>

(82.)

<meta>catchword: Har-</meta>

You will rectify <note>to "rectify" in alchemy means to purify a liquid through repeated distillation</note> it if you place it in a **glass gourd** <term>cucurbita vitrea</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass vessel used as the base of a still</note> under an **alembic** <term>alembico</term> <note>the "hood" or cap of the still that collects and cools vapors</note>, with a receiver vessel placed before it, into very cold spring water or water cooled with snow; you must cover the cap of the still itself on all sides with a linen cloth soaked in warmer water. For through **antiperistasis** <term>ἀντιπερίστασιν</term> <note>a philosophical concept where one quality is intensified by being surrounded by its opposite; here, the heat of the cap and the cold of the water work together to separate the substances</note>, those thinner and more subtle parts are carried upward while the watery parts are left at the bottom. This spirit of wine, I say, having been infused into the gold—which, as I mentioned, was **calcined** <note>reduced to a powder through intense heat</note>—and distilled again through the alembic, leaves behind in the bottom that same reddish color: namely, **potable gold** <term>aurum potabile</term>. It is by this substance that the physician is said to have kept himself and others safe and healthy for many years. He was accustomed, after first purging the body, to pour one **drachm** <term>drachmam</term> <note>an ancient unit of weight, roughly 3.9 grams</note> of it upon the shaved and warmed crown of the head, and to provide just as much to be drunk in **Malvasia wine** <note>a sweet, potent Mediterranean wine highly prized in the Renaissance</note>. Nor indeed does this method of preparation seem so unreasonable, since experience confirms it, and **Geber**, the prince of chemists, asserts in Book 3, Chapter 36: *All metallic bodies truly calcined are dissolved by the repetition of calcination*, adding the reason that all calcined things approach the nature of salts or alums. This method of making potable gold was greatly approved by the Noble and Magnificent **Count Julius of...** <note>The text likely refers to Count Julius of Hardeck, based on the catchword "Har-"</note>

Hardeck, etc., Marshal of our most Serene Prince and my greatest **patron** <term>Mecænas</term> <note>original: "Mecænas mihi summus"; a reference to Gaius Maecenas, the Roman patron of Virgil and Horace, here used to denote a generous financial supporter of alchemical research</note>, yields to no one in the expenses he incurs and the diligent study he applies to seeking out those more secret **remedies prepared by fire** <note>original: "remedia, igne parata"; this refers to spagyric or chemical medicines created through distillation and sublimation</note>. He was especially pleased with this method over others, as it offered greater hope for safe use and for benefiting human health, because that **golden liquor** <note>original: "liquor ille aureus"; another term for potable gold</note> was prepared without the addition of any other **corrosive** <note>original: "corrosivo"; strong acids like aqua regia were typically used to dissolve gold, but were considered "corrosive" and dangerous if not properly removed</note>, as others are accustomed to doing. Therefore, he saw to it that the work was undertaken; having progressed for twenty weeks, it consumed two hundred large **ells** <note>original: "ulnas"; a historical unit of measurement, here likely describing the volume or length of the stacks of wood used as fuel</note>, each containing four cubits of wood.

   But by some unknown fate, it happened that the **craftsman** <term>artifex</term> of the work passed away, leaving the task unfinished. Although the Magnificent Count at first neglected it as a destroyed and incomplete work, after some days he was drawn by a desire to see it. Having removed the small vessels, he found certain **iron tools** <term>ferramenta</term>—which had been prepared for supporting the **furnace** <term>fornace</term>—liberally scattered with **golden spots** <note>original: "aureis maculis"</note>. These spots appeared on both sides of the tools, even though they were thicker than a broad finger; thus, one could see that those drops falling from the plates had penetrated through the entire iron. Indeed, one may see those very tools to this day...

...it was; for this reason, it was preserved by the Magnificent Count. It had been sent as a gift to His Highness from the Curtine Mountains.

Indeed, in its transparent and shining color, it resembled the purest gold as much as anything could, and it was a most pleasant sight to behold. Yet, in its taste, we detected nothing except **spirit of wine** <term>spiritus vini</term> <note>original: "spiritum vini"; distilled alcohol or ethanol, often used as a solvent in alchemical preparations</note>, from which the craftsmen themselves admit they produced it. But (regrettably), its effects on the diseases for which we tested it did not live up to the promises made by the authors. 

There are countless similar methods for preparing "golden waters" from wine in the volumes of **Raymond Lull** <note>Raymundus Lullius (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher and polymath; many alchemical works were pseudonymously attributed to him in the later Middle Ages and Renaissance</note>. ...I have experienced the method of preparation through myself and others, but it is a task of great and long-lasting labor to correctly draw out and separate those subtler parts from the watery and earthy ones; this is what the **chemists** <term>Chymici</term> <note>original: "Chymici"; referring to early modern alchemists or chemical philosophers who sought to understand the composition of matter</note> call the "separation of elements." For neither wine by itself, nor its spirit (unless the gold has perhaps been prepared by means of salts), no matter how much color it might receive from the gold, will ever convert the gold's entire substance into water, unless the wine itself acquires the highest sharpness and saltiness through a long-lasting fire,

...or even its own foul and terrestrial **dregs** <term>fæx</term> <note>original: "fæx"; the sediment or dregs left after a chemical process, often considered the "earthy" element in alchemy</note>, of which I spoke above, may be converted into the nature of salt by long-lasting **calcination** <term>calcinatione</term> <note>original: "calcinatione"; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to oxidize it or remove volatile components</note> (as **Raymond** <note>Referring to Raymond Lully (c. 1232–1315), a major figure in medieval alchemy and philosophy</note> abundantly teaches), having been whitened and burned out. Therefore, all the methods of preparation mentioned by me so far agree in this: that gold is either dissolved by the salts themselves, or by such things which, having been burned for a long time by the force of fire, acquire something of the nature of salt (as all burnt things usually do), by which they act upon the metals themselves.

This is also clearly testified by **Geber** <note>Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), often called the "Father of Chemistry," whose works were foundational to European alchemy</note> in Book 1, chapter 22, saying: *Everything that is dissolved must necessarily have the nature of salt or **alum** <term>aluminis</term> <note>original: "aluminis"; a type of mineral salt used as an astringent and in chemical processing</note>, or their like; nor are other things found to be dissolved besides those.* Therefore, whatever things are dissolved must be dissolved through that same nature—that is, they must be reduced to water.

However, since the greater part of these mineral salts—and especially the "waters" or acids prepared from them—are most foul in taste and smell, horrible, corrosive, and abominable to human nature, most practitioners until now have seemed to be the wisest of all, and thought they had performed the work best, who prepared it in such a way that it had the least amount of corrosion in itself and could be administered within the body as safely as possible. Carefully considering these things within my own mind, I came to...

(87.)

I finally came to this opinion, since experience teaches that the use of **common salt** <term>salis communis</term> <note>original: "salis communis"; ordinary table salt (sodium chloride)</note> is not only not harmful to the human race, but very pleasant, and indeed so necessary that no food would please us without it; and if someone should abstain from it for too long, they would easily fall into diseases arising from **putrefaction** <term>putredine</term> <note>original: "putredine"; the process of decay or rotting, which early scientists believed was a primary cause of disease</note>, just as meat and most other moister foods, unless they are preserved by it, are extremely prone to putrefaction. 

Since these things are so, I came to the hope that perhaps by that same common salt, **gold** <term>aurum</term> could be reduced into "water" <note>This refers to the alchemical "solution of gold," a liquid form of the metal intended for medicinal use</note> perhaps even more successfully and just as easily as with other salts. And so, while I was attempting this matter—which I have always been most eager to investigate—in various ways through myself and through others whose labor I used for this purpose, and after seeking in vain and fruitlessly for several years, a path finally offered itself by which the matter succeeded quite happily. 

For since I have, in my valley of **Blavia** <note>Likely referring to the Bělá valley in the Sudetes mountain range</note>, where I have my estates and workshops in which **iron** <term>ferrum</term> is smelted and plates are hammered, and where among other things I have built a **laboratory** <term>laboratorium</term> (as the **chemists** <term>Chymici</term> <note>original: "Chymici"; referring to alchemists or early chemical practitioners</note> call it) for this purpose—this place, situated in the mountains (which **Ptolemy** <term>Ptolomæus</term> <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the ancient geographer whose maps were still authoritative in this period</note> calls the **Sudetes** <term>sudetas</term>), is most suitable for preparing this because of the abundance of forests and charcoal, and at the same time the easier opportunity for purchasing glassware and pottery...

Indeed, I have used it often with the most happy success (by the grace of the gods) in long-term diseases, putrid conditions, and various obstructions. Because, besides the fact that it is not too sharp and does not burn, it is highly penetrative. Therefore, it is effective for **apoplexy** <term>Apoplexiæ</term> <note>original: "Apoplexiæ"; a sudden loss of consciousness, similar to a stroke</note>, **paralysis** <term>paralysi</term> of both the tongue and other parts, **spasms**, the retention of menses and urine, and the **stone** <term>calculo</term> <note>original: "calculo"; kidney or bladder stones</note>—which it wonderfully breaks down—as well as **jaundice** <term>icterytiæ</term> and the onset of **dropsy** <term>hydropis</term> <note>original: "hydropis"; edema or swelling caused by excess fluid</note>, by opening [the body's passages], and by restoring the appetite and the stomach...

<meta>This page continues the author’s transition from describing the clinical effects of his remedy to revealing its preparation. ...[that this work] has perfectly succeeded for me, I ask that you direct all your strength and energy toward this with me. Since your writings carry great weight with many, strive to draw the minds of others to this as well, so that everyone might exert their best efforts to investigate and bring back into the light this **Medicine** <term>Medicinæ</term> so highly praised by the ancients. We must ensure that this supreme remedy, of which we have been deprived for so long, does not fall into contempt and remain known to us in name only. 

To complete this work, three things are primarily required (besides the vessel and the furnace): **gold** <term>aurum</term>, **common salt** <term>sal commune</term>, and a very small quantity of **distilled vinegar** <term>aceti stillatitij</term> <note>vinegar that has been purified through distillation to increase its acidity and potency</note>. 

Before the gold is used in the work, it must be most precisely purified and separated from other impure metals with which it is mixed. **Antimony** <term>Antimonium</term> <note>a brittle, silvery-white mineral; in alchemy, it was known as the "wolf of metals" because it "devoured" impurities in gold during smelting</note> performs this excellently if they are melted and flowed together, provided that the weight of the **stibium** <term>stybij</term> <note>another name for antimony</note> far exceeds the gold, even by tenfold.

...is found pure. Also, so that the **vinegar** <term>acetum</term> may be more suitable for the work, it must be **distilled** <term>distillandum</term> in this manner: 

Take two pounds of the sharpest wine vinegar and one pound of **common salt** <term>salis communis</term>—specifically that which is brought to us from **Halle of the Hermunduri** <note>modern-day Halle an der Saale, Germany; the Hermunduri were an ancient Germanic tribe</note> on the **river Saale** <term>Sala fluvium</term>, which is more suitable for this work than any other. Once mixed, let them be distilled through an **alembic** <term>alembicum</term> <note>an early chemical still consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</note> in a **water bath** <term>balneo Mariae</term> <note>literally "Mary’s Bath" (bain-marie); a method of heating a container by surrounding it with hot water to ensure a gentle, even temperature</note>, from which nothing but water will drip. 

For the opposite happens with vinegar as with wine. Just as when distilling wine, those more subtle **spirits** <term>spiritus</term> <note>the volatile, active essences of a substance</note> rise and flow out first with a light fire, and the water follows afterward; so, on the contrary, vinegar releases its water at the beginning with less fire, but eventually requires a much greater fire to be carried upward. 

After all the water has been separated in the way I have described, the remainder of the vinegar must be distilled through ashes <note>likely referring to an "ash bath," a method of heating where the vessel is buried in hot ashes to provide a higher, more sustained temperature than a water bath</note> with a stronger fire. However, not all of it should be drawn off to the very end, for it becomes cloudy and smells of **burning** <term>adustionem</term> <note>a scorched or charred odor caused by excessive heat</note>. Instead, as soon as a third or a half has been collected, you must stop the forced heating and discard the rest. 

Finally, pouring one pound of the **Halle salt** <term>salis Halensis</term> [as I said] into a very small amount of vinegar, stirring constantly, [seal] it well in an earthen vessel...

<summary>This page provides specific instructions for distilling wine vinegar and preparing it with a specialized salt from Halle to create a potent chemical solvent. The author explains the unique distillation curve of vinegar compared to wine, emphasizing the need for different heat levels to separate water from the active acidic spirit.</summary>

<keywords>distillation, vinegar, common salt, Halle an der Saale, alembic, bain-marie, spirits, chemical preparation, laboratory technique</keywords>

...having been placed in a warm spot, let it be slowly dried, and let this be repeated a third or fourth time. Furthermore, to every pound of **common salt** <note>original: "salis communis"</note> mix one or two ounces of this acid salt. In this way, you will have the salt prepared for **distillation** <note>original: "destillationi"</note>. 

The **furnace** <term>Fornax</term> should be similar to those we use to draw out the waters which they call "**strong waters**" <note>original: "aquas fortes"; this typically refers to nitric acid, a powerful solvent used in metallurgy and alchemy</note>. In this furnace, the fire should surround the **cucurbit** <term>cucurbitam</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass or ceramic vessel used as the base of a still</note> on all sides. 

I, however, have always added another, taller section to the back of the furnace; because it saves the practitioner from the constant watchfulness and labor of adding charcoal, artists foolishly call it the **Lazy Henry** <term>pigrum Heinricum</term> <note>Also known as an athanor, this was a self-feeding furnace designed to maintain a constant heat for long periods without manual intervention</note>. 

Among the vessels, the most suitable is the **cucurbit** <term>cucurbita</term> which, because it has a wide belly and a long, narrow, curved neck, is called a **retort** <term>retorta</term> by **Chemists** <term>Chymicis</term>. This vessel must be protected on all sides with "lute" <note>original: "limo"; a paste made of clay, manure, or sand used to seal vessels and protect glass from breaking under high heat</note> so that it may more easily withstand the force of the fire. 

A similar glass vessel should be placed at the front to receive the liquid, but it should be very large, capable of holding two pounds of distilled water. Let the middle part of the earthenware **retort** <term>retortæ</term> be filled with the salt prepared as I have described. Then, once it is placed crosswise in the furnace as is customary, let the other vessel containing water be attached to the outside. 

For the first four hours, the fire should be very light; then, it should be gradually increased until smoke or white clouds, like waves, [appear] in the cucurbit...

...so that they are seen to enter from the **beak of the retort** <note>original: "ex retortæ rostro"; the beak is the long, downward-pointing neck of the distillation vessel</note>.

Then, gradually drive out the **spirits** <term>spiritus</term> <note>volatile vapors or essences released during heating</note> by increasing the fire more and more until the **retort** finally glows red-hot. Thus, the fire should be maintained for three days—or even longer, for the longer the better—without concern if neither drops nor other visible spirits are seen rising. For although none may be visible, one will nevertheless see a certain **white powder like chalk** <note>original: "pulveris albi ceu cretæ"</note> clinging day by day to the bottom of the water and the walls of the glass. 

When I, in order to collect more of that **white earth** <term>terra alba</term>, maintained the heat for a month and a half, I observed it being deposited and increasing daily. And what is truly marvelous: every day, for about half an hour (though the exact time varied), such a pleasing and fragrant **odor** was perceived—not only in the room where the work was being done, but even from a distance, and sometimes throughout the entire house. This scent was not inferior in its grace even to the finest **musk** <note>original: "moscho"; a highly prized and potent aromatic substance</note>. I, along with other Magnificent Lords who (as you know) are my witnesses in this matter, often observed this with great admiration. 

This earth is moreover so...

These are partly floating and partly adhering to the glass walls; they are **transparent** <term>diaphanos</term>, very sweet, and resistant to fire. For they melt, yet do not evaporate in the fire. These should then be removed with a small wooden spoon full of holes—such as cooks use for **skimming meat** <note>original: "despumandis carnibus"</note>—twice a day, or as often as they have solidified...

Moreover, even when mixed in a small quantity, it adds a much greater sweetness to other things than common sugar.

Specifically, the one is solid and somewhat more **fixed** <note>in alchemy, a "fixed" substance is stable and does not evaporate or change when exposed to fire</note>, which, when cast upon molten metals, perfects them; but the other—called the **Elixir of Life** <term>Elixir vitae</term> by **Raymond** <note>original: "Raymundo"; referring to Raymond Lully (Ramon Llull), a 13th-century polymath whose name was frequently attached to influential alchemical texts</note>—is dissolved into a water suitable for drinking, preserves youth, and restores strength.

However, it is certain from my own experience and that of others—nor can anyone who handles these matters correctly deny it—that most of these so-called waters do indeed contribute and achieve something in changing **silver** <term>argentum</term> into **gold** <term>aurum</term>, and to a sufficient degree.

Yet, I have not yet seen them brought to that fuller perfection which the chemists promise.

For although they may transform metals, they nevertheless barely repay and exceed their expenses and labors with a small profit. This proves that some degree of perfection is still lacking in them.

<meta>This page continues from page 100. On the previous page, the author discussed how alchemical waters (the Elixir) might transform metals like silver into gold, but expressed skepticism because they do not yet seem to achieve the "fuller perfection" promised by chemical authors.</meta>

# 101
M 5

As for the other point—namely, the warding off of diseases and the preservation of prosperous health—waters similar to the **Water of Life** <term>aqua vitae</term> <note>original: "aquæ vitæ"; literally "water of life," a term used for distilled spirits or medicinal elixirs believed to prolong life</note> (as I have said) have indeed often been of great benefit when used with the proper method. However, I have so far seen none that could cure all diseases indiscriminately, or restore fallen teeth and fingernails <note>the regrowth of teeth and nails was a common claim in alchemical literature regarding the "rejuvenation" of the body</note>. This shows (unless, as I fear, the **chemists** <term>chymistae</term> <note>early modern alchemists</note> indulge too much in their praises in this matter, as they usually do in most things) that something is still lacking for their perfection.

Whence, indeed, who might not reasonably doubt whether the **potable gold** <term>aurum potabile</term> <note>original: "aurum potabile"; a legendary medicine consisting of gold dissolved in liquid, believed to be a universal cure-all</note> of the ancients was true and perfect? Nevertheless, I do not believe its use should be entirely discarded. For since the powder of gold is so highly commended by **Avicenna** <note>original: "Avicennæ"; Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the Persian physician whose medical encyclopedia was a standard European textbook for centuries</note> and other physicians for ailments of the heart and other diseases, and **Arnold** <note>original: "Arnoldus"; likely Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist</note> so greatly praises the infusion of gold and its quenching in wine, it will doubtless perform far more if it is reduced into parts so subtle that they can penetrate all the veins—and especially if this is achieved without **corrosion** <note>original: "rasione"; likely referring to the use of harsh, corrosive chemicals to break down the metal, which would make it dangerous to ingest</note>.

### (102.)

There is another mark of true **potable gold** <term>aurum potabile</term> <note>original: "auri potabilis"; a legendary medicinal elixir consisting of gold dissolved in liquid</note> that must by no means be neglected: even if it is mixed with imperfect metals and perfects them, gold itself should never be able to be recovered from the liquid. This recovery is easily done with other waters that merely stain things with color (such as the one I mentioned above, which we saw staining things with a saffron color in the house of the most illustrious Doctor Gallus, of noble memory). 

For if spring water is poured in—to which others mix a little **tartar** <note>original: "Tartari"; a chemical substance (potassium bitartrate) often collected from the sides of wine barrels and used in alchemical processes</note>—and as the water gradually evaporates upwards, the gold powder ought to settle at the bottom. However, if the elixir is prepared correctly, or if it has ascended through an **alembic** <term>alembic</term> <note>original: "alembicum"; a classic distillation apparatus used by early chemists to purify liquids</note>, this precipitation does not happen at all.

These things are written to you, most illustrious **Mattioli** <note>original: "Matthiole"; Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a renowned Italian physician and botanist to whom this letter or text is addressed</note>, as I said, not for the purpose of teaching you, nor so that I might prefer my opinion over others who perhaps have a different judgment on these matters.

Nevertheless, I do not believe these secrets of nature—which can contribute greatly to **medical use** <term>medicus usus</term> and provide others with the opportunity to investigate them more deeply—should be despised or discarded. Truly, those **Zoili** <note>original: "Zoili"; plural of Zoilus, a Greek grammarian known as a harsh and cynical critic. In this period, the term was used for anyone who spitefully criticized the hard work of others</note> are uniquely detestable, who attack good men when, to provide for the life and utility of mortals, they [strive] most...

<summary>The author describes a test for "true" potable gold, arguing it should be chemically inseparable from its liquid form, and defends the study of alchemical medicine against cynical critics.</summary>

...[bestow the] greatest diligence, care, labor, and expense in learning these **more secret medicines of nature** <term>naturae secretioribus medicinis</term> <note>original: "naturæ ſecretioribus Medicinis"; the author refers to spagyric or alchemical remedies that were often hidden from the uninitiated</note>. And if, by chance, things do not always succeed in every part according to their wishes, [critics] never cease to enviously mock, bite, and pick at them; and yet, in the meantime, they do not provide anything better in their place.

But since in this unhappy age of ours, even the labors and most successful efforts of the most distinguished men do not escape the "bites" of these critics, is it any wonder that when these tasks cannot be easily perfected by others according to their desires, they are criticized and torn apart by **slanders** <note>original: "calumniis"</note>? (For most people criticize what is unknown to them).

There is another class of envious men—a "useless weight upon the earth"—who are no less deserving of blame here. These are people who, through their own talent, labor, and industry, produce something of value, yet are driven partly by **ambition** <note>original: "ambitione"</note> so they might excel above all others, and partly by **greed** <note>original: "avaritia"</note> so they might turn a profit.

Sometimes this also happens through ignorance of the **ancient writers** <note>original: "veterum scriptorum"; referring to classical authorities in medicine and natural philosophy like Galen or Dioscorides</note>, as if the same things were not...

...[claiming] that much better things were handed down by the ancients, they keep and hide them as a secret and almost as a mystery in their own private treasury, and do not bring them forth for public use. However, **blessed are those who held the middle ground!** <note>original: "Medium autem tenuêre beati!"; a variation of the Roman proverb suggesting that virtue and happiness are found in moderation and balance, here applied to Mattioli’s balance of private study and public service</note> You, therefore, most distinguished Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577) was a renowned Italian physician and botanist, famous for his commentaries on Dioscorides</note>, are deservedly most blessed; you are most worthy of everyone’s attention, favor, and goodwill.

For you are so far from "turning up your nose" at <note>original: "naso suspendere"; a classical Latin idiom meaning to mock or treat someone with contempt</note>, disapproving of, or slandering the hard labors of others in **chemical** <term>chymicis</term> <note>original: "chymicis"; refers to "chymistry," the early modern pursuit of distilling and refining substances for medicinal use</note> investigations, that you even have **furnaces** <note>original: "fornaces"; the specialized ovens used in a laboratory for distillation and chemical reactions</note> constructed in your own home. With these, whenever you have leisure time, you diligently occupy yourself. You have made such progress with them that the waters you prepare—which they call **waters of life** <term>aquas vitæ</term> <note>original: "aquas vitæ"; literally "waters of life," referring to distilled spirits or medicinal elixirs believed to refresh the "vital spirit" or life force of the patient</note> because they restore the vital spirit—are daily in the hands of our Most Serene Prince as well as other **Bohemian** <note>original: "Bohemorum"; referring to the aristocracy of the Kingdom of Bohemia, where Mattioli served as a physician</note> Lords, and are rightly regarded as the highest form of remedy.

Indeed, there is no need for me to speak of your faithfulness in publishing your discoveries for public use, since your own famous writings abundantly testify to it. For you, being **full of cracks** <note>original: "plenus rimarum"; a humorous idiom from the Roman playwright Terence, usually meaning someone who cannot keep a secret. The author uses it as a compliment to say Mattioli is incapable of hiding helpful medical secrets from the world</note>...

(to use the words of Terence <note>original: "ut cum Terentio loquar"; likely a reference to the Roman playwright Terence's character who described himself as being "full of holes" or unable to keep a secret, here used as a compliment to Mattioli’s openness</note>) it flows out in every direction.

Evidence of this, among other things, is the use of **calcined antimony** <term>stybij calcinati</term> <note>original: "stybij calcinati"; antimony roasted or heated into a powder, used in early modern medicine as an emetic or purifier</note>; while others strove to hide this as a most profound secret, you were the first (so far as I know) to make it known in your writings.

I come in the certain hope that if you believe any of this will help advance medical science and its understanding, you will confirm it with your precise and sharp judgment. That you will do this candidly, honestly, and generously, I do not doubt in the least given our friendship; and by the affection I hold for you and for the sake of the public medical benefit, I pray and beseech you. Farewell. Prague.

# § (106.) §

This letter teaches many good chemical techniques and procedures, among which the **preparation of spirit of wine** <term>praeparatio spiritus vini</term> <note>original: "præparation spiritus vini"; high-proof alcohol produced by distillation, often used as a solvent in alchemy</note> via <unclear>avhweist</unclear> is well worth noting. The main reason I have inserted it here, which motivated me, is the **preparation of sweet crystals from common salt** <term>praeparatio cristallorum dulcium ex sale communi</term>, which is described quite clearly therein, not without a special **praise** <note>original: "encomio"; a formal expression of high praise or a eulogy for the substance's virtues</note> for it.

Indeed, other practitioners **distill** vinegar from salt three times, always using fresh salt, so that more of the volatile spirits of the salt may be incorporated into the vinegar. 

Here, however, the **Author** mentions only one **distillation**. Furthermore, the four **imbibitions** <note>original: "imbibitiones"; an alchemical process where a solid substance is repeatedly soaked in a liquid until it absorbs it or becomes "saturated"</note> of fresh salt with the **impregnated vinegar** <term>impregnated aceto</term> are set up for a month; one is processed each week in a **Waldenburg butter jar** <note>A specific type of stoneware vessel from Waldenburg, Saxony, prized by chemists for its ability to withstand heat</note> with its corresponding lid, using gentle heat, so that the **more volatile spirits of the salt** <term>volatiliores salis spiritus</term> may gradually **fix** <note>original: "figiren"; the alchemical process of turning a volatile, vaporous, or liquid substance into a solid state that can withstand fire</note> themselves. The more often and longer the imbibition takes place, the better it is. 

Likewise, one is not bound to use the salt from Halle in Saxony, but may instead use **rock salt** <term>sal gemmae</term> <note>original: "Sal gemmae"; literally "salt of the gem," referring to pure, crystalline mineral salt</note> or some other good salt (but not the Tyrolean salt...

### (107.)

...sooty <note>original: "rische"; likely completing "rußige" from the previous page</note> (because it carries a very fixed **lime-spirit** <term>lime-spirit</term> <note>original: "Kalck-Geist"; an alkaline essence believed to reside in calcined materials like lime or ashes</note> within it). In place of the **imbibition** <note>original: "imbibition"; the process of soaking a solid in a liquid until it is saturated</note>, some have **dissolved** <note>original: "dissolvirt"</note> a well-purified salt in **distilled vinegar** <note>original: "aceto destillato"</note> and **rotted** it <note>original: "putrificirt"; an alchemical term for allowing a substance to ferment or "digest" at a gentle, steady heat</note> **for a month** <note>original: "per mensem"</note>; thereafter, they drew off the vinegar **through an alembic** <note>original: "per alembicum"; a distillation head</note>, though not entirely to dryness (in which extraction I found that the **spirit of vinegar** <note>original: "Spiritus aceti"</note>, contrary to its nature and habit, passes over first, before its **phlegm** <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: "phlegmate"; the watery, tasteless, and medically inactive part of a distilled liquid</note>). 

Then, the remaining moist salt is placed into a **retort** <note>original: "retorten"; a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</note> and mixed with well-burnt or **calcined pebbles** <term>calcined pebbles</term> <note>original: "calcinirte Kißlingstein"; flint or quartz stones heated to high temperatures until they become brittle, used to increase surface area or aid the reaction</note>—the more the better—and with **distilled water** <note>original: "aquam destillatam"</note> placed in the receiver, it is finally **distilled** **according to the proper method of the art** <note>original: "secundum artis justam methodum"</note> in a **lazy Heinz** <term>lazy Heinz</term> <note>original: "faulen Heinzen"; a specialized furnace, also known as an Athanor, designed to provide steady heat over long periods without the chemist needing to constantly tend the fire</note>.

In summary, one must see to it that the moist **spirit of salt** <note>original: "Spiritus salis"; what we now call hydrochloric acid</note> is **constrained** <note>original: "constringirt"</note> within the salt through a secret **coagulating power**, which they first found to be quite useful in distilled vinegar. But it is even better if they add to the salt (**for example** <note>original: "exempli gratia"</note>, to nine pounds of beautiful white salt, half a pound of finely crushed) **tartar** <note>original: "Weinsteins"</note>, or even, **in place of tartar** <note>original: "loco tartari"</note>, the soot from the chimney...

### (108.)

...added as its own <note>original: "als seinen," completing the thought from the previous page</note>, moistened **once or several times** <note>original: "semel vel pluries"</note> with good vinegar and **dried** <note>original: "exiccirt"</note>, and then distilled. But it is best that one **dissolves** <note>original: "dissolvirt"</note> **litharge** <term>litharge</term> <note>original: "lithargirium"; lead monoxide (PbO), a yellowish-red mineral</note>, or calcined **Saturn** <term>Saturn</term> <note>the alchemical name for lead</note> (which metal **Ripley** <note>George Ripley, a prominent 15th-century English alchemist</note> calls the **"Tartar from the black grapes of Catalonia"** <note>original: "Tartarum ex nigris uvis Cataloniæ"; a poetic alchemical code for lead-based substances</note>) in vinegar, and performs the often-mentioned **soaking** <note>original: "imbibition"</note> of common salt with it.

Furthermore, it should be known that to promote the **solidification** <note>original: "congelation"; here referring to the condensation of the acidic vapors</note> of the **spirit of salt** <term>spirit of salt</term> <note>original: "Saltz-Geistes"; hydrochloric acid gas or liquid</note>, some have placed "air-water," or a **spirit of wine or whey** <note>original: "Spiritus vini vel serum lactis"</note> into the **receiver** <term>receiver</term> <note>original: "Recipientem"; the glass vessel used to collect the distilled liquid</note> instead of common water. This **spirit** or **whey** should be poured through unslaked, powdered **lime** <note>original: "Kalck"</note>, in the same manner as one usually makes lye; or when the **spirit of wine** <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>original: "Weingeist"; alcohol or ethanol</note> has been **redistilled** <note>original: "cohobirt"; the process of pouring a distillate back over its residue and distilling it again to increase its "virtue"</note> at least nine times from the distilled oil and the thick, dreggy **crude tartar**, or **soot**, or even from the distilled **"Microcosmic Rebis"** <term>Microcosmic Rebis</term> <note>original: "Rebis Microcosmici"; a term referring to the "dual matter" of the human body, typically human urine or feces in a laboratory context</note> itself, and has received a **fiery impression** from it. The **burning spirit of Saturn** <term>burning spirit of Saturn</term> <note>original: "Spiritus ardens Saturni"; likely acetone, produced by the dry distillation of lead acetate</note>, distilled from its **sugar or gum** <note>original: "saccharo vel gummate"; referring to lead acetate, also known as "sugar of lead" for its sweet taste</note>, is the best for this purpose, but also the most precious.

Whichever of the mentioned **spirits** <term>spirit</term> <note>original: "spiritus"; in alchemy, a volatile substance or distilled essence</note> it may be, each one has the power—though one more than the others—to **constrict** <note>original: "constringiren"; to thicken or solidify</note> the mineral moist spirits and to change them into a **dry substance**. This is seen in **dried vitriol** <note>original: "victriolo exiccato"; iron or copper sulfate that has been heated to remove its water content</note>, which, when **soaked** <note>original: "imbibirt"</note> and **digested** <term>digested</term> <note>the process of gentle heating over a long period</note> with such a spirit for a **philosophical month** <term>philosophical month</term> <note>original: "mensem philosophicum"; a traditional alchemical period of forty days</note>, then distilled and **sublimated** <term>sublimated</term> <note>heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again</note>, results in the **spirit of vitriol** rising up in a **congealed** form. It appears in the shape of a **white sulfur**, which is also called the **"true sulfur of all the Sages"**—sought and desired by many, but found and obtained by very few.

For whoever does not understand the **congealing power** <note>original: "vim congelativam"</note> which dwells most strongly in the aforementioned subjects may well abandon the **preparation** of these **secrets** <term>arcanen</term> <note>original: "arcanen"; from the Latin 'arcanum,' meaning a hidden or profound medicinal mystery</note>. ### IT BEGINS

# EPISTLE

## By an Anonymous Author, on the Principles of the **Hermetic art** <term>Hermetic art</term> <note>original: "artis Hermeticæ"; a traditional name for alchemy, named after the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus.</note>.

Since it is a common custom, not only among the ancient observers and seekers of the secrets of the **art of Alchemy** <term>art of Alchemy</term> <note>original: "artis Alchymiæ"; the medieval study of transforming matter, often aimed at creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> but also among the moderns, to debate whether the common **mineral gold** <term>mineral gold</term> <note>original: "aurum minerale vulgi"; the physical metal used for money or jewelry, which alchemists distinguished from their own "philosophical" substances.</note> is the true intention of the Philosophers: I too shall speak sincerely regarding what I think of this matter—namely, that it is not—demonstrating this by the authorities of the Philosophers. For **Senior** <note>original: "Senior"; referring to Zadith ben Hamuel, a 10th-century Arabian alchemist whose work was foundational to Western alchemy.</note> says clearly enough: *Our gold is not the common gold.* And **Constantinus** <note>original: "Constantinus"; likely Constantinus Africanus, a monk and scholar who translated many medical and alchemical works into Latin.</note> adds: *Our gold is the mother of gold, and the root of all metals.*

I proceed; the **Philosophers' Gold** <term>Philosophers' Gold</term> <note>original: "Aurum Philosophorum"; the essential, "living" substance sought by alchemists, rather than the "dead" metal found in nature.</note> is white or black in its actual state, and red in its potential. This white or black is im-
<meta>catchword: per-</meta>

<summary>This page introduces an anonymous treatise clarifying that the "gold" sought by alchemists is not the common physical metal, but a "philosophical" substance that serves as the root of all metals. It cites historical authorities like Senior and Constantinus to support this fundamental distinction.</summary>

<keywords>Hermetic art, Alchemy, Philosophers' Gold, Senior Zadith, Constantinus Africanus, mineral gold, philosophical principles</keywords>

(117.)
N 5

is perfect and is perfected by the **perfect red** <note>original: "rubeo perfecto"; refers to the final stage of the alchemical Great Work, the Rubedo or "Reddening."</note>.
Concerning which perfect red **Anaxagoras** <note>Anaxagoras (c. 500–428 BC) was a Pre-Socratic Greek philosopher who suggested that the sun was a mass of red-hot metal.</note> said: the sun is a burning red stone, and **HERMES** <note>referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> agrees with this, saying that without the red stone no true **tincture** <term>tincture</term> <note>original: "tinctura"; a medicinal or alchemical substance capable of transforming and "coloring" other metals into gold.</note> is made.
From this it is clear that without both kinds of gold, the work of **Alchemy** <term>Alchemy</term> <note>original: "Alchymiæ"; the art of transmutation and purification.</note> is not completed, and that there are two types of gold: one, namely, the white or similar kind, and the other red. Concerning which **Rosinus** <note>original: "Rosinus"; likely a Latinization of Zosimos of Panopolis, an influential 3rd-century alchemist.</note> says: *Unless you place gold in gold, you have nothing.*
But regarding gold in potential, **Rasis** <note>original: "Rasis"; refers to Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi (865–925 AD), a Persian physician and alchemist.</note> says in the book *On the Perfect Mastery* <note>original: "de perfecto Magisterio"</note> that it is a neutral body, neither sick nor healthy.
But you, who serve in this so praiseworthy science, should draw out the rose shining among thorns like the Sun, and invoke the divine help of the **Holy Spirit** <note>original: "Spiritus Sancti"; many alchemists believed spiritual purity and divine grace were required to succeed in the laboratory.</note>, and diligently examine the qualities and internal **complexions** <term>complexions</term> <note>original: "complexiones"; the specific balance of the four qualities—hot, cold, moist, and dry—that determined the nature of a substance.</note> placed in secret, and you shall not go astray.

**Mercury** <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurius"; here referring to "Philosophical Mercury," the essential fluid principle of all metals, not just the liquid metal in a flask.</note> in its manifest form is white, cold, and moist; but in its hidden form it is red, as well as hot and dry. This cannot be the common mercury, for **HERMES** said the true Mercury of the Philosophers is extracted from the body of **ADROP** <term>ADROP</term> <note>original: "ADROP"; a cryptic alchemical term, often interpreted as the "lead of the philosophers" or the raw, unrefined starting material of the Stone.</note>, whose manifest

(118.)

...is cold and dry; but the internal is hot and moist.

**Lead** is a base body among the foolish, but of great value among the **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to alchemists—those who seek the hidden secrets of nature and the transformation of matter.</note>. Because everything that is external is contrary to its internal nature. Therefore, because its external **complexion** <term>complexion</term> <note>original: "complexio"; refers to the specific balance of the four primary qualities—hot, cold, moist, and dry—that define a substance's physical and spiritual character.</note> is cold and dry, its internal nature will be hot and moist. Now, everything that **tints** <note>original: "tingens"; refers to the "tincture" or the power to transform the color and nature of a base metal into gold.</note> into gold and coagulates **Mercury** is of the genus of the **Sulfur of gold** <note>original: "Sulphuris auri"; in alchemical theory, Sulfur represents the active, masculine principle of fixity and color, while Mercury represents the passive, feminine principle of fluidity.</note>. Because Sulfur, which is in gold, has congealed its own **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum"; the liquid, volatile form of mercury.</note>. But it is evident by experiment that the internal Sulfur of lead, when manifested from its hidden state, coagulates Mercury into gold; therefore it is of the nature of the Sulfur of gold.

Besides this common lead of the masses, there is another certain substance which is called the **Lead of the Philosophers**, and it is **Magnesia** <note>Not to be confused with the modern element magnesium; in alchemy, Magnesia was a cryptic term for the "maternal" raw material of the Stone, often described as a dark mineral containing hidden light.</note>. Now, Magnesia is a "black earth with white eyes" <note>A classic alchemical metaphor describing a dark ore (like antimony or galena) that contains sparkling, metallic, or crystalline inclusions.</note>. Moreover, such a black earth is leaden **Marcasite** <term>Marcasite</term> <note>original: "Marcasita"; a historical term used for various metallic sulfides, including iron pyrites and ores of bismuth or antimony.</note>. And leaden Marcasite is **Antimony**. Therefore, **Antimony** is that "lead" of which the Philosophers speak. From which is extracted **vegetable quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum vegetabile"; "vegetable" in this sense does not mean "from a plant," but rather "living" or "capable of growth," referring to a mercury that has been "animated" or purified for the Work.</note>.

<summary>This page reveals that the "Lead of the Philosophers" is actually Antimony (Magnesia), explaining that while its exterior appears base, its "internal sulfur" possesses the power to transform mercury into gold. It emphasizes the alchemical principle that a substance's internal qualities are often the exact opposite of its outward appearance.</summary>

(119.)

...the red in color, holding the **secret of secrets** <note>original: "arcana arcanorum"; a common alchemical phrase referring to the ultimate mystery or the most hidden knowledge of the craft.</note>. For this **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum"; liquid mercury, considered the "mother" or the transformative fluid of all metals.</note> is the **Sulfur of lead** in its nature; and this Sulfur of lead is, in its internal essence, the **Sulfur of gold**. This **quicksilver of the Philosophers** <note>Alchemists distinguished between common mercury found in shops and "Philosophical Mercury," a purified or specialized substance used in the "Great Work."</note> decays in heat and moisture; from it, the elements are separated and then joined together according to their weights, and the **Philosopher’s Stone** is extracted from it. 

Such a stone possesses a swift transformation within its own kind. For although the same stone can be extracted from **aqua vitae** <term>aqua vitae</term> <note>literally "water of life"; while modern readers know this as brandy or high-proof alcohol, alchemists used the term for various powerful distilled solvents believed to hold life-giving properties.</note>, it requires a much longer time and possesses less efficacy. Because of this, many **Philosophers** have worked with wine or *aqua vitae* alone, while many others have worked with **Antimony** <note>A brittle, silvery-gray metalloid that alchemists believed held the key to purifying gold and extracting its essence.</note> alone. 

But here lies a greater secret: **Antimony** alone generates the **Great Stone** from its own natures—that is, from the four elements. This, however, requires a long measure of time. If it is made from Antimony and dissolved gold, it is perfected more quickly, yet it is of less efficacy. Nevertheless, when made from Antimony together with dissolved gold and silver, the **Stone** is perfected in eighty days. Among those who possess this knowledge, it is called the **Quintessence of Antimony** <term>Quintessence</term> <note>original: "Quinta essentia"; the "fifth essence" or the purest, most concentrated spiritual core of a substance, beyond the four physical elements.</note> and the "light of our lead," possessing the nature of both quicksilver and the sulfur of gold.

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

It compares different starting materials, noting that while Antimony mixed with gold and silver is faster, Antimony alone produces the most powerful "Great Stone."</summary>

(120.)

h s 2

But let us return to the **sulfur of common lead** <note>original: "sulphur plumbi vulgaris"</note>. This is prepared either from its **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum"; mercury. Alchemists believed every metal was composed of a specific "quicksilver" and "sulfur" that could be separated and recombined.</note>, or from a purer part of its ore, by precipitating and fixing the one or the other through various degrees of fire, until that blackish **Mercurial whiteness** <note>original: "albedo illa nigricans Mercurialis"; refers to the *albedo* or "whitening" stage of the Great Work, which follows the initial blackening (*nigredo*).</note>, which is now visible, is hidden; and the hidden **redness of the sulfur** <note>original: "sulphuris rubedo"; the *rubedo* is the final stage of the alchemical process, representing the perfection of the Stone.</note> is made manifest. 

Then extract this manifested sulfur with **very sharp vinegar** <note>original: "aceto acerrimo"; likely distilled vinegar or a potent acidic solvent used to pull essences out of raw materials.</note>, and mix it with the **essential spirits** of any minerals; and they will return more quickly to the nature of earth and be fixed. If you soak this same sulfur several times with the **fixed water of reddened vitriol** <term>vitriol</term> <note>a class of sulfate minerals; "reddened vitriol" (colcothar) is the iron oxide residue left after distillation, prized for its drying and fixing properties.</note>, until it flows in the fire like a fixed ointment; then you will coagulate **common mercury** with it into true **gold**.

From the **quicksilver extracted from iron**, a noble work can also be made, though it is diminished compared to the nobility of the quicksilver from lead. Because this [lead-mercury] is hot and moist; while that [iron-mercury] is cold and moist in its intrinsic quality. Hence, the union of the **sulfur extracted from lead** with the **quicksilver extracted from iron** is that which the **Philosophers** have concealed. Since the intrinsic nature of **Mars** <note>original: "Martis"; the planetary name for Iron. The text cuts off here, likely continuing with the properties of iron's internal essence.</note>

...compared is the **Moon** <note>original: "Luna"; silver</note>, and the inner essence of **Saturn** <note>original: "Saturni"; lead</note> is the **Sun** <note>original: "sol"; gold</note>. And this operation excels all operations, because they can be refined into infinity.

From dissolved **Gold** combined with dissolved **Silver**, a **marriage** <note>alchemical union of opposites</note> is made, and a **medicine of a minor kind** <note>original: "medicina parvi generis"; a lesser elixir or tincture compared to the Great Work</note>. Because it is necessary for there to be **waters** there that hold back the combustion of the fire; and though these may multiply the **tinctures**, their management is nonetheless dangerous.

Instead, a long **sublimation** <note>the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then condenses back into a solid</note> is required; yet the management of the fire is not dangerous, and all these things are the **secrets of the Philosophers**.

Know, moreover, that the conjunction of the **two luminaries** <note>original: "luminarium"; the Sun (Gold) and Moon (Silver)</note> with **sharp waters** <note>acids or potent corrosive solvents</note> is the **first work**, lasting twenty days with the proper management of the fire. The **second work** is in the **Mercury of Antimony** or of **wine** <note>likely "spirit of wine" or distilled alcohol used as a solvent</note>, and without a **ferment** <note>a substance added to "leaven" or catalyze the alchemical transformation</note>, it is a long work; with a ferment, it is a work of 80 days. The **third work** is taken from the conjunction of the **Mercury of Mars** <note>iron</note> and the **Sulfur of Saturn**...

In **common Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio vulgari"; quicksilver</note> there are various operations, some of which are longer or shorter than others. If you wish to proceed by the **dry path** <note>original: "siccâ viâ"; a method of alchemical transformation using high heat and crucibles rather than liquid solvents</note>, you must use its **precipitation** <note>the process of causing a substance to settle out of a solution as a solid</note>.

However, the one performed with a **ferment** <term>an additive that triggers or accelerates the transformation of a substance, much like yeast in bread</term> is short. It is the work for those wishing to finish quickly, and the task is completed in a short time and with little expense...

It is a matter of a long time and of most dangerous labor.

If we wish to work with **sulfur**, it is better to work with the **sulfur of the Philosophers** <note>original: "sulphure Philoſophorum"; a purified or metaphorical principle of sulfur, rather than the common mineral</note>, such as **Roman vitriol** <note>original: "vitriolo Romano"; typically referring to iron or copper sulfate crystals</note>, whose fixed redness we call the "fire of sulfur." Unless an easier way of working with it, known to the most sharp-witted craftsmen, becomes known to you, do not entangle yourself too much in it.

But do not concern yourself with salts on their own, even though there may be a preparation of common salt capable of **congealing Mercury** <note>turning liquid quicksilver into a solid</note>. We have experienced it thus, if you wish to prove it: Take **burnt copper** <note>original: "æs ustum"; copper that has been oxidized by intense heat</note>, well and perfectly calcined, and saturate it with the **Oil of Duenech** <note>a specialized alchemical term for the oil or acid derived from vitriol</note>, that is, of **green vitriol** <term>iron(II) sulfate; a common mineral used to create powerful acidic "oils" in the laboratory</term>, seven times, as much as it is able to drink, always roasting and reducing it. Afterwards, cause it to descend <note>likely referring to a process of casting the material down or a specific type of distillation</note>. Pure gold will descend, and the green copper will have been made red, like a clear garnet. Indeed, such a great **redness** <note>original: "rubedo"</note> descends with it that it requires some addition of **quicksilver** <term>mercury</term>, by which it is tinted with the truest color of gold. But before it descends into the form of compact gold, it is called gold...

## Having explained the Epistle of Principles.

### BEGINS

# Exposition and practice of the Stone of Adrop, collected from the book of Pliny the Philosopher, which is entitled: The Philosophers’ Aromatic, Treasury and Secret of Secrets.

I wish to declare openly how our **corrosive water** <note>original: "aqua corrosiva"; a potent solvent or acid used to break down metallic substances</note> ought to be prepared, which is a great secret. The composition of this water has never before this time been so clearly declared in any philosophical book; without it, our medicine never achieves its effect.

Therefore, take as much as you wish of the **urine of boys** <note>A common source of ammonia and nitrogenous compounds in early laboratory practice</note> in good health, 14 years of age, very well purified of dregs, and from it make a **lye** <note>original: "lixivium"; an alkaline solution</note> with **potash** <note>original: "cineribus Clavellatis"; literally "clubbed ashes," used to produce potassium carbonate</note> and **quicklime** <note>original: "calce viva"; calcium oxide, used for its caustic properties</note>. Then distill it through a filter

<summary>This page introduces the "Stone of Adrop" and begins the practical instructions for creating a "corrosive water," a fundamental alchemical solvent made from urine, potash, and quicklime.</summary>

<keywords>Stone of Adrop, corrosive water, Pliny the Philosopher, Secret of Secrets, alchemy, potash, quicklime, lye, urine of boys</keywords>

O 2

...for the third time, and afterward congeal it over a slow fire, and you will find at the bottom of the vessel a white and hard salt, of a grayish color. Dissolve that salt upon a porphyry stone <note>original: "porphyrio"; a hard stone used as a surface for grinding or absorbing moisture</note> in a damp place, and distill it through an alembic <note>A type of distilling apparatus consisting of a gourd-shaped vessel and a cooling cap</note>; it is then called our **corrosive water** <note>original: "aqua nostra corrosiva"</note> and the **Vinegar of the Philosophers** <note>original: "acetum Philosophorum"</note>, which we use for our blessed work.

Therefore, take our incorruptible body **ADROP** <note>A term often used in alchemical texts to signify the raw material of the Work, specifically lead</note>, named **Saturn** in Greek <note>original: "Saturnus"; the alchemical and astrological name for the metal lead</note>, and shape it into thin plates; then place it in a glass vessel and add to that body some of our corrosive water. Seal the mouth of the vessel well with **philosophical lute** <note>original: "luto philosophico"; a specialized airtight sealant used to prevent vapors from escaping</note> made of **Armenian bole** <note>A reddish medicinal clay used as a binder</note>, **quicklime** <note>original: "calce viva"; calcium oxide</note>, egg whites, and powdered glass mixed in equal parts. Cook it with our fire, which is made from **horse dung** <note>original: "stercoribus caballorum"; fermenting manure provided a low, steady, "moist" heat preferred for certain alchemical processes</note>, because without this fire, according to all the Philosophers, the **Great Work** <note>original: "Magisterium"; the mastery or completion of the alchemical process</note> cannot be perfected. And certainly, this fire is moist and dark, and it prevents the stone from burning and preserves it. Cook the aforementioned stone until it is broken down with the help of the aforementioned corrosive water. For within a natural day <note>A full 24-hour period</note>, the body is reduced into the **prime matter** <note>original: "primam materiam"; the original, undifferentiated substance from which all things are formed</note>.

**Vocabulary:**
Adrop (Lead), Saturn (Lead), corrosive water, Vinegar of the Philosophers, philosophical lute, Armenian bole, quicklime, horse dung, Great Work, prime matter.

...is led. Then cook the first matter <term>materia prima: the raw, chaotic base material required to begin the Great Work</term> until it is transmuted into black ashes. And this will occur within 20 natural days. Then grind and wash those black ashes, and cook them with the aforementioned water repeatedly, until they are more fully converted into a grayish color, which is the sign of perfect fixation <term>fixation: the process by which a volatile substance is rendered stable under heat</term>. Afterward, increase the fire slightly with warm urine <note>Urine was commonly used by alchemists as a source of ammonia and salts</note> until the aforementioned ashes become of a more intense whiteness.

A short time before this more intense whiteness, the peacock’s tail <note>original: "color pavonis"; a celebrated stage in the alchemical process where a rainbow of colors appears on the surface of the matter, signaling the transition from the "black" stage to the "white" stage</note> will wonderfully appear. This color is the crown of your heart, and the end of the white stone <term>Lapis albus: the White Stone, or White Elixir, a substance believed to be able to transmute base metals into silver</term> nobly fulfilled. And then you have the perfect medicine named the **White Elixir**.

And know, my son, that once a small amount of the material is held, the **White Stone** can be perfected and completed within a Philosophical year, which is commonly called a month—which is a great secret of this art. Because of the ease of the work itself and the shortness of its duration, this medicine is called *Women’s Work and Children’s Play* <note>original: "Opus mulierum & ludus puerorum"; a common alchemical trope implying that once the correct "fire" is found, the process proceeds easily on its own</note>, which whoever once possesses shall never want for anything further. And the total expenses do not exceed thirty pence <note>original: "triginta denariorum"; emphasizing that the value of the work comes from the operator's skill rather than the cost of the ingredients</note>. And just as Saturn is the highest of all the planets: so...

And this stone, when it has been perfectly prepared, is the most precious of all stones. This stone is called the **height of this world** <note>original: "altitudo hujus mundi"; this suggests the pinnacle of physical and spiritual achievement in the alchemical art</note>, and it possesses a power above all the powers of all other stones. The material of this blessed stone is an **incorruptible body** <term>corpus incorruptibile: a purified substance that has been purged of all impurities and can no longer be destroyed by fire</term>, which is semi-fixed and soundless <note>original: "insonabile"; likely referring to the substance being "dead" or "mute," lacking the metallic ring of common metals like copper or silver</note>, killing <note>original: "mortificans"; in alchemy, to "kill" a substance is to strip away its external metallic properties to reach its inner essence</note> and coagulating **Mercury** <term>Mercurium: quicksilver</term> with its very odor.
